#3082. Want to Become a Stock Market Millionaire? $250 a Month in This ETF Could Get You There.

This could be the anchor of a great stock portfolio.

There are many ways to become a millionaire through the stock market, and some are riskier than others. But if you have $10,000 to start and you can invest $250 monthly, you can get there in 30 years with minimized risk.

One of the lowest-risk ways to achieve millionaire status is by investing in an index fund that tracks the S&P 500. However, you'd need to invest a lot of money or wait a very long time.

Instead, or in addition, you could invest in an exchange-traded fund (ETF) that tracks a growth index, like the Vanguard S&P 500 Growth ETF (VOOG +0.91%). It's similar to the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.30%), but it focuses on the growth companies in the index. Instead of 500 holdings, it has 152. That's a riskier setup, but it's still well diversified.

Over time, that means it may be more susceptible to declines when the market falls, but it can gain much more when the market does well. And over many years, since bull markets tend to be much longer than bear markets, the growth ETF has outperformed.

Since inception in September 2010, VOOG has returned an annualized gain of 16.4%, edging out VOO's 14.8%, and it's actually the highest annualized gain since the inception of any Vanguard fund outside of two that were created last year.

Over a longer period, the S&P 500's annualized gain is around 10.9%, so let's assume a 12% annualized gain for the growth ETF over the next 30 years. If you start with $10,000 and add $250 per month, which amounts to a $3,000 annual investment, you'll have more than $1 million in 30 years.

Jennifer Saibil has positions in Vanguard Admiral Funds-Vanguard S&P 500 Growth ETF and Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3077. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3071. Where Will Lemonade Stock Be in 5 Years?

Growth is accelerating, but the stock hasn't followed.

Lemonade (LMND -4.65%) stock has performed poorly this year despite the company growing fast. It's down 36% this year, but it just announced its twlevth consecutive quarter with accelerating top-line growth.

There's only so long the market can remain pessimistic about this stock. At some point, smart investors will recognize the opportunity, and the price will start to climb again. Here's where it could be in five years from now.

Lemonade is an insurance technology company that has been using artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning throughout its operations since its inception more than a decade ago. It created its business with a digital infrastructure that underpins everything it does, and management claims that the interconnected parts that communicate give it an edge over incumbents.

Today, all of the insurance companies are moving toward digital and AI, but they also have legacy systems in place are very difficult to simply replace or move into new technology.

The company is attracting loads of new customers who are looking for a better experience, such as digital onboarding and claims without having to speak to an agent, and there has been spectacular growth. In the third quarter, in-force premium (IFP), the average total amount of premiums, increased 34% year over year. Premium per customer increased 9%, and total customers increased 23% to more than 3.5 million.

Management noted that as AI agents start taking over tasks, having one find an insurance policy works in its favor. Customers may tend to consider brand names important, while AI agents will look for the best price and coverage. CEO Daniel Schrieber called it a "potentially transformative shift in insurance distribution."

The company is also still rolling out, providing a tremendous growth runway for the next few years. Its renters product is now live in every state, and it has launched 25 new product-geography combinations so far in 2026. Its Car product is now live in 14 states, reaching half of the U.S. population.

From a coverage perspective, as it reaches new regions, its growth prospects scale. Each region represents multiple new opportunities from different kinds of policies and bundles. That means even if it doesn't reach the second half of the population over the next five years, its top line could still double, or more. If IFP growth continues to accelerate and grow at a compound annual growth rate of 35%, for example, IFP would grow from $1.5 billion to $6.7 billion.

Lemonade still isn't profitable, but it's on track to reach positive net income soon.

For several years, its loss ratio was too high, keeping investors away. The loss ratio measures how much of a policy is paid out as claims, and if it's too high, the insurance company won't make money. It's now competitively low, which means the company's algorithms are working and its underwriting is effective. As of the third quarter, it has a low 12-month trailing loss ratio of 60%.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) loss improved from $26 million last year to $20 million this year, and management maintains that it will be positive in the fourth quarter, with a full year of positive adjusted EBITDA in 2027.

At one point a few years ago, management said that it expected to have positive net income in 2027, but it has not recently renewed that guidance. However, it's likely to happen within the next few years.

In five years, Lemonade is likely to be operating a thriving, profitable business that could capture market share from the insurance powerhouses. For example, Lemonade's trailing 12-month revenue is less than a tenth of Progressive's (PGR -2.32%), but it's growing much faster. The stock should follow.

Jennifer Saibil has positions in Lemonade. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Lemonade. The Motley Fool recommends Progressive. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3063. 3 Reasons Why Ultra High Yield Realty Income Is a Buy After Its Latest Earnings Report

Realty Income's third-quarter 2026 earnings showed that rising rates aren't the end of the world for this giant net lease REIT.

Shares of Realty Income (O -0.77%) have been under pressure since the pandemic. Even after a recent uptick, they are down roughly 25% from their 2020 high. But the real estate investment trust (REIT) hasn't really changed much over that span. If anything, the company has become more attractive, with its third-quarter 2026 earnings underscoring that.

Indeed, despite rising interest rates, it was another fairly boring quarter, with a modest increase to the company's guidance and yet another dividend increase. Here's why dividend lovers should still view Realty Income as a foundational investment.

"Boring is good" is in quotes because those are the words of Realty Income's CFO, Jonathan Pong. They came up during a conversation about the potential impact of rising interest rates on the giant net-lease real estate investment trust (REIT). That comment basically summarized the entire ethos of Realty Income, which trademarked the nickname "The Monthly Dividend Company." You have to be reliable, to the point of being boring, to back up a nickname like that.

The third quarter of 2026 was another quarter in which Realty Income quietly went about its business, even as real estate markets faced headwinds. It's true that rising rates will increase the cost of capital, but that didn't stop Realty Income from finding a way to make $2.6 billion in profitable investments (with a 170-basis-point spread).

There are several reasons for this success. First, Realty Income is massive, with a globally diversified portfolio of over 15,500 properties. It sees almost every significant deal. Second, it has an investment-grade-rated balance sheet, which, along with its size, affords it advantageous access to capital markets. And, third, the REIT has also been expanding its sources of funding, from strategic property sales (capital recycling) to the creation of an institutional asset management business. There are more points that could be made here, but the big story is that Realty Income wasn't waiting for rates to rise before addressing the issue; it was already prepared. Which is exactly what you'd expect from a company that's bold enough to call itself "The Monthly Dividend Company."

Realty Income had a solid quarter, which is good news. But it also raised its full-year guidance, which basically tells the market that the REIT is operating just as well as it always has. The raise was modest, taking the adjusted funds from operations (FFO) guidance range from $4.44 to $4.45 per share up to, wait for it, $4.45 to $4.46. That's not a shocking change, but remember: "boring is good."

Also worth noting, the company maintained its occupancy outlook at 98.5%, which is very strong. Same-store rent growth remained at 1.1%-1.3%. And management reaffirmed the goal of hitting $10 billion in acquisitions, noting it was already at $7.9 billion, roughly 80% of the way there. The guidance raise was good news; the fact that Realty Income's business is progressing in line with expectations is probably even better news.

There's one more easy-to-overlook event from the quarter that dividend investors will find important: Realty Income increased its dividend. The hike was modest, with the dividend rising just 0.7% year over year. However, the dividend has been increased annually for 32 years and is generally hiked a little bit every quarter. The adjusted FFO payout ratio is a very reasonable 73%.

It probably shouldn't be a shock to anyone that "The Monthly Dividend Company" has a well-supported and growing dividend. But it is one more piece of the dividend story that this quarter's earnings highlight, and it shouldn't be ignored. Slow and steady is what Realty Income is going for. Sure, that's kind of boring, but as Jonathan Pong pointed out, boring is good.

Nothing that happened in the third quarter of 2026 changes Realty Income's long-term story. That's the big takeaway: this giant REIT was built from the ground up to survive difficult periods. With a 5.6% yield, it can provide a solid backbone to a diversified income portfolio, allowing you to add lower-yielding but higher-growth stocks to the mix. Realty Income is worth buying after its third-quarter earnings because its earnings proved, again, that this ultra-high-yield REIT isn't likely to surprise you (or keep you up at night worrying).

Reuben Gregg Brewer has positions in Realty Income. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Realty Income. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3059. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#3058. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3057. Should You Buy VOO Before the Market Rebounds?

The sharp post-election pullback in the S&P 500 has investors reeling. It might be a good buying opportunity.

The stock market just gave investors something they haven't really seen since the start of the Iran war: a chance to buy at a discount.

The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +0.30%) has fallen roughly 6% from its October high through the close on Nov. 5 following a sharp post-election sell-off. For investors who thought stocks were getting too expensive, this could be a real opportunity.

But then there's the issue of whether investors are ready to take advantage of it. Are they going to view it as a chance to buy on sale given their long-term focus? Or are they going to panic and look at selling stocks instead of buying?

If you're a long-term investor, this could be a buying opportunity you haven't seen since April.

The midterm elections gave Democrats control of both chambers of Congress, raising some questions about the future of the artificial intelligence (AI) boom.

Will there be efforts to slow the level of innovation? Will there be potential restrictions on data center construction? Will it be more challenging to spend billions of dollars to expand AI infrastructure?

That's a legitimate short-term concern for Vanguard S&P 500 ETF investors. Mega-cap tech stocks have unquestionably driven the market's gains over the past few years. By some measures, their valuations leave little room for disappointment. The election may have simply been the catalyst to let investors realize those fears.

Corporate earnings growth hasn't yet given investors much reason to get out of stocks. According to FactSet, S&P 500 companies were on pace to deliver roughly 30% year-over-year earnings growth in Q3. That growth rate is important because it's currently helping offset several other potentially negative factors.

Inflation remains elevated. Oil prices are still above $90 per barrel. And long-term Treasury yields are putting pressure on balance sheets and equity valuations. Without strong earnings growth, the market would have a much harder time justifying its current prices.

For now, however, earnings are providing that support. The risk is that AI spending slows and earnings expectations follow. That'll be the most important factor over the next couple of quarters. But we have yet to see broad evidence that a slowdown is occurring.

If you're a long-term investor, I don't think recent events are a justification to alter your asset allocation.

The current pullback is large enough that it's a good opportunity to buy shares at a reasonable discount, especially considering that the macro backdrop hasn't really changed. There's fear about what could happen. But not much about what will change.

The market may or may not be at its short-term bottom here. But a 5% dip has been rare so far this year. And corrections have a recent history of reversing quickly. As long as corporate earnings continue growing, I think the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF remains a solid long-term buy.

David Dierking has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends FactSet Research Systems and Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3055. Breakfast News: The Buffett Moat Test

Does Amazon Web Services still have a moat? Test it against Buffett and Munger’s criteria with this week’s prompt.

Welcome to our Sunday Edition: Your weekly step toward better investing through AI.

Does Amazon Web Services still have a moat, and what would Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger make of it?

Amazon (AMZN -0.35%)’s AWS (Amazon Web Services) launched in 2006 and spent years as the clear leader in cloud computing. Artificial intelligence (AI) has made the field more crowded. Microsoft (MSFT -0.99%) sells access to OpenAI’s models through Azure, Alphabet (GOOG +0.78%) rents out its own TPU chips, and companies like CoreWeave (CRWV +6.28%) have built businesses renting Nvidia (NVDA +2.77%) GPUs by the hour. Meanwhile, all the big cloud providers are spending tens of billions of dollars a year on data centers.

For Amazon shareholders, that raises two questions. Are AWS’s advantages widening or narrowing? And is all that spending building a lasting edge, or is it simply the cost of staying in the race?

This week’s prompt looks at both. It starts with a standard industry analysis of the cloud market, then tests Amazon against the investing principles Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger laid out in decades of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKB -0.25%) shareholder letters and annual meetings. It doesn’t guess what either man would do. Munger died in 2023, and Buffett is no longer Berkshire’s CEO. It applies their written criteria, which are strict enough that few companies pass all of them.

If you own Amazon, or have been thinking about it, give the prompt below a try.

The cash flow question deserves the most attention. Buffett has written about his wariness of businesses that need heavy reinvestment just to hold their position. Whether AI data centers fit that description is still debated, and the prompt asks your assistant to back its answer with filings and figures.

AI Power Tip: Deep Research mode does noticeably better on Part 2, where the peer table needs fresh, segment-level figures from four different companies’ filings. Ask your assistant to show its sources for each cell.

This prompt is an educational tool, not a recommendation. The output is generated by your AI assistant, not by The Motley Fool, and may contain errors. Valuation figures in particular rest on assumptions your assistant chooses, so treat them as a way to see what the price implies, not as a target. Always verify key figures yourself.

You are an equity research analyst. Analyze Amazon’s competitive advantage, focusing on AWS as its hyperscale cloud business, with Amazon-wide context where it matters (e.g., retail scale, custom silicon, logistics, advertising cash flows). Your tone is inspired by The Motley Fool’s approachable tone and education focus but you are not The Motley Fool, you do not speak for it, your output is not Motley Fool research, and you do not provide financial advice.

PART 1: PORTER’S FIVE FORCES (hyperscale cloud market)

For each force, give a rating (Low / Medium / High), 2-3 evidence-backed points, and a one-line "so what" for Amazon:

- Bargaining power of suppliers (e.g., Nvidia/AMD, chips, power, data center land)

- Threat of substitutes (e.g., on-prem, private cloud, neoclouds like CoreWeave)

Identify Amazon’s top 3 hyperscaler competitors by latest reported cloud revenue (verify with the most recent quarter/fiscal year; likely Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud, and Alibaba Cloud, but confirm). Build a table comparing AWS to each on:

- Cloud revenue, YoY growth, and market share

- Operating margin (cloud segment where disclosed)

- Custom silicon (Trainium/Inferentia vs. Maia vs. TPU vs. others)

- AI model and platform strategy (e.g., Bedrock vs. Azure OpenAI vs. Vertex AI)

- Enterprise lock-in and ecosystem breadth

- Classify each of Amazon’s advantages as a durable moat or an eroding one, by type: scale economies, switching costs, network effects, cost position, or intangible assets.

- Name the biggest threat to AWS over the next 3-5 years.

- Give a bottom-line verdict in 3 sentences.

Evaluate Amazon through the documented investing principles of Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger (Berkshire shareholder letters, annual meetings, Poor Charlie’s Almanack). This is a framework, not a prediction of what either would actually do. Munger passed away in 2023 and Buffett is no longer Berkshire’s CEO, so frame it as "how their stated criteria would apply."

A. Four-filter test (Pass / Borderline / Fail, with a one-line justification each):

- Circle of competence: Is the business understandable and predictable over 10+ years?

- Durable moat: Does AWS earn high returns on capital that competitors can’t easily erode? Reference your Part 3 findings.

- Management: Capital allocation record, owner orientation, and candor.

B. Munger-style inversion: What could permanently impair this investment? Consider AI capex intensity (Buffett has historically disliked businesses that must reinvest heavily just to stay competitive), margin pressure from custom-silicon races, regulatory risk, and customer concentration in AI workloads. Does free cash flow, after the current capex surge, support the earnings story?

- Calculate owner earnings (net income + D&A − maintenance capex only). State your estimate of maintenance vs. growth capex and flag it as a judgment.

- Build a conservative DCF or owner-earnings-yield valuation with three scenarios (bear / base / bull), stating every key assumption (growth, margins, capex, discount rate, terminal value).

- (i) Estimated intrinsic value per share range

- (ii) A "would-buy-below" price using a 25-30% margin of safety on the base case

- (iii) The current share price (verify date) and the growth/margin expectations it implies (reverse DCF)

- (iv) Verdict: Below buy price / Fair value / Above fair value

D. Verdict in 3 sentences: Would a Buffett/Munger-style investor be interested, at what price, and what single fact would change the answer?

- Use the latest available data and cite sources with dates. Flag any figure that is estimated or not directly disclosed.

- Cite Buffett/Munger principles to specific, verifiable sources where possible. Do not fabricate quotes.

- Flag any statement about Berkshire’s actual Amazon holdings as needing verification against the latest 13F.

- Keep the total under 1,500 words, with the Part 2 table as the centerpiece.

- End with: "This is analysis, not investment advice."

You are inspired by an approachable, education-focused tone. You are not The Motley Fool, and you do not provide financial advice.

Clear disclosure: Every response must include this statement: "Educational only—this is not financial advice. Consider consulting a fiduciary for personal recommendations."

If the user changes their stated preferences or goals mid-conversation, acknowledge the change and adjust your response accordingly.

Never claim the result is a definitive measure of someone’s true risk tolerance.

Present any score or output as an educational framework based on the user’s answers.

Never provide personalized financial advice, buy/sell instructions, or specific securities.

If the user asks for personalized investment advice, restate the educational-only role and provide general educational ranges or concepts instead.

Look at where the four filters disagree. AWS could pass the moat test and fail on price, or look cheap on earnings while capital spending absorbs most of its free cash flow. The reverse DCF (discounted cash flow) in Part 4 is worth reading closely, too. It works backward from today’s share price to show what growth and margins investors are already assuming, which is easier to check than any single estimate of intrinsic value.

Rerun the prompt after each earnings report, since capital spending guidance, AWS growth, and segment margins change every quarter. You can also swap in Microsoft and Azure, or Alphabet and Google Cloud, to see how the other big providers do on the same tests.

Did your AI assistant’s read on Amazon’s moat match your own?

#3053. Elon Musk Says Tesla Will End Cybertruck Production -- Here's Why

Car and Driver calls the Tesla Cybertruck "dead."

Tesla (TSLA +4.23%) CEO Elon Musk formally unveiled the Cybertruck in 2019. Actual production didn't begin until 2024. Next year, production will formally end, according to a new tweet from Musk.

Musk shared on social media that the electric vehicle (EV) maker will wind down "production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year."

Why is the Cybertruck going extinct? Slow sales are largely to blame. According to estimates, Tesla is only selling a few thousand units per quarter. If the Cybertruck were a mass success like the Model 3 and Model Y, it likely would not be getting the axe.

Tesla CEO Elon Musk. Image source: The White House.

But there's another obvious reason that Tesla is winding down production for one of its least successful models ever.

Tesla's $1.4 trillion valuation isn't based on its success as an automaker. Instead, the market is pricing in plenty of growth for other opportunities, namely AI, humanoid robots, and self-driving cars. All of these initiatives will be capital-intensive to scale. Freeing up capital by ending a low-volume, low-potential model is a wise decision for Tesla as it pivots its priorities toward the future.

Just as importantly, Tesla will then be able to use the factory space to focus on Cybercab production. Musk said so directly, tweeting that ceasing Cybertruck production is designed specifically "to make room for increased Cybercab production."

Some experts believe that the robotaxi market will eventually be valued at $8 trillion to $10 trillion globally. Success in that market can move the needle for Tesla in a way Cybertruck demand never could.

Ryan Vanzo has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3051. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#3046. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3043. These 3 Unstoppable ETFs Could Build a Million-Dollar Portfolio With Little Effort on Your Part. Here's How.

It just takes two things to make your money grow.

It may sound hyperbolic, but you could build a million-dollar portfolio with just a few investments if you are patient and persistent.

When I say patient, I mean like 20 or 25 years of patience. And when I say persistent, I mean regularly contributing money to those investments.

And rather than racking your brain doing deep analysis and research to find the right stocks and then wondering if they are the right choices through bouts of short-term volatility, you can do it pretty simply and cleanly with a few exchange-traded funds (ETFs).

The beauty of ETFs is that they trade like stocks, yet they are diversified baskets of dozens, hundreds, or even thousands of stocks. So if the markets do get volatile, a good ETF can reduce that risk through diversification, whereas a portfolio of just a few individual stocks is more exposed to wild swings.

So, you could actually be well diversified with just three ETFs and have the long-term alpha to build a million-dollar portfolio over time. Those three ETFs are the Invesco QQQ Trust (QQQ +1.40%), the Vanguard Information Technology ETF (VGT +1.35%), and the iShares Russell 1000 (IWB +0.38%).

The Invesco QQQ invests in the stocks in the Nasdaq-100 -- the 100 largest non-financial sector stocks. It is synonymous with tech stocks, since they dominate the Nasdaq, but it is somewhat diversified, with 16% in consumer discretionary stocks, 6% in telecoms, 5% in healthcare, and 3% in industrials.

Currently, the top three stocks are Nvidia, Apple, and Microsoft.

The Invesco QQQ Trust has a stellar long-term track record, returning about 13% per year over the past 25 years, on average. So, if you invested $5,000 in the QQQ and averaged a 13% return over the next 25 years, contributing $100 to that investment every month, you would have about $304,000 today.

If you had another $5,000 to invest, another great ETF to buy and hold for the long term is the Vanguard Information Technology ETF. The technology sector has been the best-performing sector over the past 30 years, and it should continue to thrive as we move into the heart of the AI boom, which could last another 10 to 20 years. But beyond that, there will no doubt be another breakthrough technology, whether it's quantum computing, robotics, or something we haven't even heard of yet, to drive the market.

What's great about the VGT ETF is that it is one of the most diversified tech ETFs. It holds 318 technology stocks across all cap sizes, from megacaps to microcaps, and tracks the MSCI US Investable Market Information Technology 25/50 Index. So its top holdings are the same as the QQQ, but it has a far broader swatch of tech stocks, tapping into the growth potential of small- and mid-sized tech stocks.

It was launched in 2004, so it has a 22-year track record. Over that time, it has averaged about a 15% annualized return. So, if you invested $5,000 and contributed $100 per month to the VGT, you would have about $437,000 across 25 years -- assuming it matches that 15% annual return.

If you had another $5,000, I'd suggest investing it in the iShares Russell 1000 ETF. I'd favor this over an S&P 500 (^GSPC +0.31%) ETF because it lets you tap into mid caps as well as large caps with the Russell 1000 ETF, giving you a bit more diversification.

And the iShares Russell 1000 ETF actually beats the State Street S&P 500 ETF (SPY +0.31%) over the past 25 years, with an average annualized return of 10.1% compared to 10% for the SPY.

So, if you did the same thing, a $5,000 investment contributing $100 per month, you'd accumulate about $177,000 over 25 years.

So add it up, and a $15,000 investment in these three ETFs would amount to roughly $918,000 in 25 years. The key is to have the patience to hold these ETFs for the long term and add to them consistently. Ultimately, you are paying yourself and investing in your future.

Investing in these three proven, diversified ETFs is a simple way to set your portfolio and forget it.

Dave Kovaleski has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Apple, Microsoft, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool recommends Nasdaq. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3034. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#3019. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#3016. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

#3014. World’s No. 1 restaurant crowned as contest comes under increasing scrutiny

Asador Etxebarri in Atxondo, Spain, is the world's top restaurant.

That's according to The World's 50 Best Restaurants, which announced results on Wednesday evening in Lima, Peru. Asador Etxebarri, helmed by chef Bittor Arguinzoniz, sits in Basque Country and features grilled items like a charred beef chop, fresh mozzarella from the chef's buffalo, or house-made chorizo.

Chef Gaggan Anand's Gaggan in Bangkok, which combines progressive Indian cuisine with French, Thai and Japanese influences, was No. 2, and Alchemist in Copenhagen, from head chef Rasmus Munk, took the third spot.

India returned to the top 50 for the first time since 2004, with Masque at No. 26 and NAAR at No. 49. Mainland China made the list for the first time since 2021, with Meet the Bund in Shanghai at No. 21 and Ru Yuan in Hangzhou at No. 36.

Chef-restaurateur Ferran Adrià, who achieved worldwide renown with his avant-garde techniques at his now-closed restaurant El Bulli, received the SevenRooms Icon Award. Adria continues to support culinary research and education through the nonprofit El Bulli Foundation.

The 50, as the umbrella organization is called, creates lists of restaurants, bars, hotels and vineyards that it ranks as top in the world or region. It draws on industry networks to help rank establishments.

For years, The 50 has been seen as a kingmaker and an opportunity for restaurants and bars worldwide. Being "on the list," many chefs and other restaurant-industry insiders have said, makes a huge difference for reservations and revenues.

But there's been increased scrutiny of the contest as well. Chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, which was ranked 60th this year, posted on Instagram that she wouldn't be attending the Lima ceremony, saying she would rather invest resources "in our guests, our team, our producers and the daily work of making the restaurant better."

While stressing that she had enjoyed a "vibrant community" at the 50 Best and made many friends from it, she said "over time, however, success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system itself," and that promotion of one's restaurant risked becoming as important as the work happening inside it.

She appears not to have been alone. Many of the world's top chefs consider awards and lists to be a tricky balance with their work at their establishment.

"Guides and lists now shape the industry they claim to assess," Ivan Brehm, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Nouri in Singapore, told CNBC. "Their judgments influence where diners spend money, where investment flows, which chefs get attention and what younger cooks learn to aspire to."

He added of the 50 that "none of this denies the brilliance of the restaurants on the list or the opportunities it has created for all of us. The question is what it sidelines and what it incentivizes."

Roš' Instagram post had more than 10,400 likes as of Friday, including from top chefs worldwide.

"The reaction and the reach were unexpected" from the post, Roš told CNBC in an email. "I did not write a post to provoke, but more as an eye-opener. And apparently a lot of people have the same perception as I do."

The 50 defended its list and role within the industry.

"The 50 does not claim to have the capabilities to police establishments on their budgets or surrounding activities," a spokesperson told CNBC. "The 50's role is to provide a snapshot of expert opinions on the best hospitality experiences in a given region or category. It does not act as a regulatory body and it applies the same voting rules and principles consistently across all countries and regions."

The time and effort put into the contest can be a dilemma, too.

"We didn't go to Lima this year. It's a huge amount of time in the air for a very short amount of time on the ground and, like Ana, I felt my time was better spent being present in our restaurant with our team and our guests," said Dave Pynt, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Burnt Ends in Singapore, who won the 50 Best Chef's Choice Award in 2017.

However, he added that "when the awards are closer and easier to get to, I love going. They're fun, you learn a lot, and you get to catch up with mates and people in the industry you don't get to see enough."

#3010. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

Disclosures: Tidal owns/holds all the securities mentioned in the article.

#3009. Democrat shaping AI policy bought chip stocks despite backing congressional trading ban

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, a Democrat helping write his party's artificial intelligence agenda, has disclosed new purchases of AI-linked stocks — just months after voting to ban lawmakers and their families from buying individual shares.

The New Jersey Democrat disclosed an Oct. 12 purchase of between $1,001 and $15,000 of Benchmark Electronics stock, according to a filing released this week. His jointly owned Morgan Stanley account also bought up to $15,000 worth of Applied Materials shares on Oct. 26, following three earlier purchases of leading AI chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices.

While there's no evidence of wrongdoing, the trades put Gottheimer's investments in the spotlight as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress in January, gaining new power to regulate and investigate the AI industry.

Gottheimer, a former Microsoft executive, sits on the House Financial Services subcommittee overseeing AI in finance, has introduced legislation on AI regulations and co-chairs a commission developing Democrats' broader AI agenda.

Gottheimer's spokesman, Haraden Bottomley, said the congressman turned over control of his investments to a third party with "full investment discretion" before taking office in 2017.

"Gottheimer is also awaiting approval from Congress of a blind trust. In the meantime, he has taken the extra step of setting up an independent trust," Bottomley said.

Democrats are already targeting the AI industry. Reps. Maxine Waters and Greg Casar introduced a resolution opposing taxpayer bailouts for AI companies, referred in part to Gottheimer's committee. Casar has also called for AI executives to testify under oath, while other Democrats are pushing to restrict data centers or halt new construction altogether.

The purchases also contrast with Gottheimer's longstanding support for restricting congressional stock trading. In July, he was among just 13 Democrats who voted for the Stop Insider Trading Act, which would prohibit lawmakers and their families from purchasing most individual stocks. The bill passed the House but has not become law. Some Democrats opposed it both because it contained a voter-ID provision and because some said it should have gone further in curbing stock purchasing and holding by elected officials.

Four years earlier, Gottheimer backed legislation requiring lawmakers to place investments in blind trusts and pledged to establish one himself.

The AI purchases are just a small slice of Gottheimer's sprawling investment portfolio. The five trades totaled between $5,005 and $75,000, compared with $15.1 million to $59 million in disclosed household assets, including up to $44.5 million in stocks. His household has reported more than 1,100 securities transactions over three years, according to Follow the Filings. Gottheimer reports household assets jointly with his wife, Marla Tusk, who is general counsel at a political consulting firm.

Advanced Micro Devices competes with Nvidia in AI chips, while Applied Materials supplies semiconductor manufacturing equipment. It was also Benchmark's largest disclosed customer in 2025, accounting for 14% of revenue, according to a securities filing.

Benchmark, meanwhile, generated 30% of its third-quarter revenue from semiconductor equipment and another 16% from advanced computing and communications, according to its Oct. 28 earnings report. Gottheimer also disclosed an Applied Materials purchase in August and a sale in September.

The purchases came shortly before a sharp selloff in AI stocks. The Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.5% Thursday, as Nvidia dropped more than 9% and AMD roughly 17%. The index includes both companies.

Benchmark fell nearly 17% over Wednesday and Thursday, closing at $59.34 — roughly 25% below its closing price on Gottheimer's October purchase date — before recovering somewhat Friday.

#3004. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient.
