#2992. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2986. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2984. Bona doubtful, no Sixers starters on injury report for Cavs game

Bona doubtful, no Sixers starters on injury report for Cavs game originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers listed backup center Adem Bona as doubtful for their Friday night meeting with the Cavaliers.

Bona's official listing on the team's Thursday evening injury report was "cervical spine sprain."

He played one of his better games so far this season Wednesday in the 8-1 Sixers' win over the Pistons, slamming in two second-quarter dunks and posting five points, two rebounds, a steal and an assist in 15 minutes.

Bona headed back to the Sixers' locker room with 8:56 left in the fourth quarter. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse told reporters postgame that the 23-year-old big man had "neck soreness" after taking a hit.

Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear) will remain out for the Sixers in Cleveland. The Sixers beat the 4-4 Cavs in dramatic fashion on Oct. 22, overcoming a 29-point deficit to win a wild home opener. If Bona were to miss Friday's game, Jabari Walker would be the logical next name in line at center behind Joel Embiid.

Notably, the Sixers included no starters on their injury report.

Tyrese Maxey had an apparent late-game injury concern Wednesday night when he seemed to hurt his right hand on an intentional Detroit foul.

However, Maxey told reporters postgame he would "be fine" and it looks like he'll be good to go against the Cavs.

#2981. Why a wave of bomb threats is proving so difficult for Europe's aviation authorities to contain

A spate of bomb threats against passenger flights has forced diversions, emergency landings and military escorts across European airspace this week, stoking fear among passengers and heaping pressure on airlines.

Authorities have described the more than two dozen threats as malicious hoaxes, and no actual attacks have been carried out. But the incidents have highlighted the huge impact that even false claims can have on a tightly scheduled aviation network.

"This is really quite exceptional, I've never seen this before in my whole career," Wilfried Covent, head of security firm Covent Consulting with a specialization in aviation, told CNBC by phone.

"We've had bomb threats in flights and towards airports, but usually maybe a single copycat, one or two max. What we see now is a systematic attack on airlines and airports."

An email sent to European broadcasters on Friday claimed to be from the leader of the group behind the plane hoaxes and also said bombs had been placed in their buildings, prompting evacuations. No bombs were found at the sites.

"What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future," the message said, according to Reuters.

The news agency said it could not verify the sender's identity, where the emails originated or whether they were linked to the threats against flights.

Covent said the "criminals" — who many security analysts say are likely to be state-backed actors working in coordination to disrupt public life for Europeans, but have not been identified — were well aware that the "weakest point of aviation is targeting it in-flight."

"With a threat on an airport or a general threat to aircraft, you can assess the situation without fully disrupting operations. When the threat is called on a specific flight number that is in the air, even with a hoax, that triggers international regulations which ensure you don't ignore it — it means a quick landing, evacuation and searches by specialist forces," he told CNBC.

"For the context of an assessment, you cannot say that 20 times before it has been a hoax so this time it is a hoax, the safety of passengers and crew is priority number one. The criminals know that."

Responsibility for dealing with hoax attacks is split, international agencies told CNBC. Assessing security threats and implementing aviation security measures primarily falls to national authorities, working in coordination with airport operators, airlines and law enforcement.

Air traffic control will also be involved in finding aircraft with an appropriate place to land, while the European Union Aviation Safety Agency, or EASA and U.K.'s Civil Aviation Authority more broadly set aviation safety rules and set how airlines prepare their crews to respond to security threats. Within the European Union, the European Commission is involved in aviation security policy and coordination between member states.

The operational decisions also depend on where the threat arises. Joji Waites, director of flight safety, policy and regulation at the British Air Line Pilots Association, said airline assessors grade a threat as red, amber or green, but that an amber verdict would never be given if the plane is airborne.

If assessors judge a threat credible, the crew is informed and the captain takes command of decisions for the aircraft, guided by company procedures. On the ground, that may mean an evacuation, while in the air the priority is to land as soon and as safely as possible.

As well as the fear and disruption to specific flights, each diversion can then affect the next flight scheduled to use that aircraft, the crew's working hours and passengers with connections.

The network effect can be significant. Philip Baum, a visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, told AP that a single diversion could cost an airline as much as $250,000, including replacement crews, missed connections and disruption to the rest of the day's schedule.

"Diversions necessitated by these bomb threats are a disturbance to a system designed for predictability and efficiency, and lead to increased workload for pilots, Air Traffic Controllers and airports," an EASA spokesperson said in emailed comments.

"As these staff have all have been trained to cope with such unexpected developments, the immediate safety threat from individual hoax threats is low. However, the higher number of diversions places an additional strain on the system."

National governments will already be looking at their aviation authority regulations and frameworks and there will be discussions at the European and global level, John Edwards, senior consultant at Gates Aviation in London, said by phone.

"Bomb threats to aviation have been going on for decades, what they'll need to be looking at is what's different. Why have there been so many, why have so many been considered sufficiently credible to warrant the kind of action that it's warranted?"

"There's a range of questions they should be able to work through fairly quickly to establish a broad-brush level of credibility. If there is some characteristic that is new, every time there is an event of this kind, it triggers a big collaboration to ensure recurrence is prevented going forward."

"In theory, he said, "the vast majority should be able to assessed and considered non-credible based on routine risk assessment processes and well established procedures."

However, Eric Schouten, a former intelligence liaison officer at the Dutch General Intelligence and Security Service and CEO of security consultancy Dyami, said that changes to existing procedural regulations would take some time and require balancing the views and responsibilities of countries, airports, safety authorities and the European Union, with geopolitics also playing a role.

"There is a high likelihood even more flights are going to be canceled or diverted. It's possible that next to be targeted could be railways and highways," he said.

"But they will figure it out. It's not the first time airlines have had to deal with hoax calls because there are always copycats."

"Whatever measures they come out with to address it, they won't share publicly" for risk of alerting the bad actors, he added.

Wilfried Covent said that while there would not be any quick changes to assessment protocol, "if this is really going on for ever and ever something will need to change or you'd have to stop flying completely."

"Based on what we've seen in the past with other kinds of threats, time goes on and the type of attack may change and move on," he continued, pointing to the wave of cyberattacks that has recently hit supermarket fridges and blood banks.

#2978. Observations after Sixers earn strong road win over Pistons, Brown scores 30

Observations after Sixers earn strong road win over Pistons, Brown scores 30 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers remain the Eastern Conference's sole leader.

They earned a 112-106 road win over the Pistons on Wednesday night to move to 8-1 on the season and stay at the top of the standings. Detroit fell to 6-2.

The Sixers last started 8-1 in the 2023-24 season, which was Nick Nurse's first as the team's head coach.

Jaylen Brown scored a season-high 30 points and pulled down seven rebounds.

Joel Embiid had 26 points and a season-best 15 rebounds. LeBron James posted 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. Tyrese Maxey added 19 points and seven assists.

The Pistons' leading scorers were Duncan Robinson with 27 points and Cade Cunningham with 26.

The Sixers were down Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Pistons' injured players included Jalen Duren (right hamstring strain) and Isaiah Joe (right hip contusion).

Nurse told reporters in Detroit he thinks "it'll be another 10 to 12 days before they kind of clear (Barlow) for getting on the court to see where he is," according to Sixers Wire's Ky Carlin.

The Sixers will visit the Cavaliers on Friday night. Here are observations on their victory over Detroit:

Embiid assisted the Sixers' first basket with a sharp pass out of a double team. He saw Brown's cut along the baseline and dished to him for a layup.

Detroit ran several double drag screen actions early, forcing Embiid to defend on the perimeter. The Pistons started 7 for 11 from the floor and took a 17-11 edge on a Robinson three-pointer.

The Sixers then made a 10-0 run and Embiid was right in the middle of it. He converted a put-back layup after a pretty high-low feed from James. Embiid hit two foul shots to give the Sixers the lead. The team raised its defensive level and Detroit's offense grew frigid.

Embiid was very good on the glass in the first half, grabbing eight rebounds. The Sixers had an excellent rebounding game as a team, limiting the Pistons to four offensive boards over 48 minutes.

Embiid didn't break out of his early-season three-point shooting slump, going 0 for 3. He's 3 for 26 (11.5 percent) on the season and hasn't made a three since the Sixers' second game.

Late in the second quarter, Embiid got into a dead-ball dust-up with Tolu Smith. After an officials' review, Embiid was assessed a technical foul for "initiating" the altercation.

Playing on his 26th birthday, Maxey missed his first four field goals and was scoreless until he hit a floater with 31.5 seconds left in the first quarter.

Brown picked up the scoring slack, tallying 10 points in the first on 4-for-6 shooting and hitting a deep, buzzer-beating three to put the Sixers up 29-21. He produced at all three levels, chose smart spots to attack and looked comfortable in the rhythm of the Sixers' offense.

Meanwhile, James did his usuall stellar work as a passer. He assisted two Adem Bona slams in the second quarter and constantly spotted open teammates. Nurse rewarded Bona's strong opening stint, playing him for a lengthy stretch.

Brown swished a corner triple to extend the Sixers' lead to 48-31. With a 3-for-5 night, Brown is at 45.7 percent from long distance so far this season. It wouldn't be surprising at all if Brown's three-point shooting improved from last season's 34.7 percent clip. With such an abundance of star power, the Sixers' hope will be that everyone gets regular open looks beyond the arc throughout the year.

As for Maxey, he sat with 3:26 left in the second quarter after being whistled for his third foul. Even with a mere two points from Maxey, the Sixers entered halftime holding a 14-point advantage.

The Pistons played better basketball to begin the third quarter.

Following a John Collins alley-oop jam, Nurse called timeout with his team's lead down to single digits. The Pistons' offense sustained some success, but the Sixers prevented a sustained run.

Embiid played an especially physical, aggressive third quarter. He tipped in his own miss and Maxey then sunk a three to make the Sixers' lead 84-68. They were up as many as 17 points in the third.

Embiid sat out the fourth quarter Monday night for the Sixers' win over the Bulls and said he was on a minutes restriction (25) because of his right foot plantar fasciitis. He played the fourth period against the Pistons and logged 33 minutes. Bona went back to the locker room early in the fourth. Nurse told reporters postgame that Bona had "neck soreness."

Detroit did not let the Sixers coast to victory.

Robinson drained a three. Cunningham made a pair of free throws and drove in for a layup that cut the Pistons' deficit to 106-102.

The Sixers halted the Pistons' run before anything disastrous was on the table. Brown blocked a Cunningham three-point try, Embiid knocked down two foul shots, and the East's No. 1 early-season team improved to 4-0 on the road.

#2977. Cowboys vs Colts: Writer predictions for Week 9

Dallas heads to Indianapolis needing an immediate response after somehow turning a 24-10 fourth-quarter lead into a 33-30 overtime defeat against Arizona. The Colts have problems of their own, losing four straight and entering Sunday at 2-6 after being demolished 42-7 by Jacksonville. This is a game where both teams desperately need something positive before their seasons begin slipping away completely.

There should be no mystery about what Indianapolis needs to try first. Jonathan Taylor has rushed for 610 yards and eight touchdowns, while Dallas just allowed Tyler Allgeier to produce an incredible 147 rushing yards on only 11 carries. After watching Allgeier break runs of 63 and 52 yards, Indianapolis would be foolish not to test the Cowboys on the ground immediately. Taylor remains the best player on this Colts offense and can also contribute as a receiver, so Dallas has to tackle far better than it did during that disastrous second half against Arizona.

The Cowboys' goal should be to stop Taylor and put the game onto Daniel Jones. Jones has thrown only five touchdowns against nine interceptions, while Indianapolis owns the NFL's worst turnover differential at minus-11. The Colts also traded All-Pro guard Quenton Nelson last week, leaving a less experienced offensive line in front of Jones.

That gives Quinnen Williams and the Dallas pass rush a real opportunity after producing five sacks against Arizona. If Dallas can force Indianapolis into obvious passing situations, this is the type of matchup where the defense should finally create pressure without constantly needing to blitz.

Dallas should look at the Indianapolis defense and see an opportunity to rediscover its running game. The Colts are allowing 4.9 yards per carry, and their defensive front no longer includes DeForest Buckner after he was traded to San Francisco. Dallas ran the ball 39 times against Arizona but gained only 93 yards, so simply calling more runs is not enough. Brian Schottenheimer needs to find the concepts and directions that actually create space for Javonte Williams rather than repeatedly running into crowded areas.

Establishing Williams would make life considerably easier for Dak Prescott. Dallas is still scoring 27 points per game, seventh in the NFL, while Prescott has 14 touchdowns against five interceptions. Indianapolis has only two interceptions all season and has just traded away its best interior pass rusher, so there should be opportunities if Dallas stays ahead of the chains.

CeeDee Lamb remains the obvious focal point, although Sauce Gardner and Charvarius Ward give Indianapolis two experienced cornerbacks capable of making the outside matchups difficult. That makes Jake Ferguson's ankle injury particularly important but he is questionable, while George Pickens was limited by a hamstring issue.

The Cowboys cannot repeat what happened late against Arizona, when the offense had chances to put the game away but failed to finish drives. Indianapolis has conceded 23 touchdowns while scoring only 15 itself, and Jacksonville just put 42 points on this defense. Dallas should attack with balance, use play-action once Williams gets going and force a Colts defense without Buckner to defend the entire field.

Maybe it's just all getting to me, but I'm starting to worry this team is fundamentally and irreparably broken. It feels good like the collapse will continue. Colts 30 Cowboys 27

The Cowboys play the Indianapolis Colts every four years, so the last win at Lucas Oil Stadium feels like a lifetime ago.

If you go back and watch that game, I think this Sunday could have a similar formula. The Colts can't get going on offense and have multiple turnovers, but stick around until the very end.

The Cowboys need to decide whether they're a pass team or a run team and stick to that identity. Flipping back and forth is what's causing this inconsistency.

It's close, but if this Cowboys team can't beat this version of Indy, all hope is lost. I think the Cowboys win 33-24.

The floundering Cowboys take on the fire sale Colts this week. As of this prediction, Jonathan Taylor is still wearing white and blue. If that holds true, he'll be the target to eliminate on Sunday. After watching Tyler Allgeier make swiss cheese of the run defense, forgive me if I lack confidence in their ability to limit the Colts running back. Still, the pattern suggests Dallas will win this one. So with a big sigh and shrug, give me the Cowboys 31-24.

I am very worried that this Cowboys team is broken upon repair. If this is the case, Sunday's matchup against the Colts could end up being the lowest point of an already very low season. I have no confidence this team will show any fight or bounce back, even in a very winnable matchup. I hope I'm wrong, but I see this game being the culmination of the downward spiral as Dallas hits an even lower rock bottom. Give me Indy, 33-20.

Dallas should bounce back this weekend (well that's what I'm hoping), and this matchup against Indianapolis gives them the perfect opportunity to do it. The Cowboys rank seventh-best in the NFL in points per game, while the Colts have the league's worst defenses, allowing 28 points per game. The Colts have also struggled offensively, with Daniel Jones throwing nine interceptions to just five touchdowns. I'm expecting a positive outlook from Dallas and a much-needed victory to get their season moving in the right direction with the Cowboys winning 31-17. Or maybe I'm just being stupidly optimistic.

#2975. America's newest small business owners with no employees are off to a hot start in AI era

It takes hundreds of billions of dollars for the tech giants to scale artificial intelligence, but it takes just a few AI tools for the smallest companies in the U.S. economy to get up and running with a cost-competitive business model.

Alex Willen expects the 12 e-commerce brands he operates with no employees to generate between $2.5 million and $3 million this year. Kristin Ginn left Microsoft in March and started trnsfrmAItn, an AI-adoption consultancy. Seven months later, she has roughly $250,000 in business received or contracted through year-end with no employees or regular contractors.

"The biggest misconception is that it's just about individual productivity; it's actually about leveraging AI to perform tasks that previously required a whole team," said Stanford economist Erik Brynjolfsson.

Early research on the impact of AI within the solopreneur business world bears watching.

According to Liya Palagashvili, a labor economist and director of the Labor Policy Project at George Mason University's Mercatus Center, new business formation is rising in direct correlation to applications unlikely to become employer firms.

In industries highly exposed to AI, business applications without early signs of hiring rose 26.8% from the first quarter of 2024 through the first quarter of 2026, from roughly 222,000 to 281,000 per quarter.

Over the same period, applications in those industries with stronger indications of eventually becoming employer businesses fell 6.4%. In construction and wholesale trade, which Palagashvili used as a comparison group, non-employer-type applications declined 0.4%.

"What AI is doing is giving those capabilities directly into the hands of the individual," she said.

Palagashvili cautioned that the findings are preliminary and descriptive and do not establish that AI caused the increase. The Census-based measure identifies applications without indicators associated with near-term payroll or hiring plans; it does not show that those businesses ultimately remained one-person firms or never hired employees.

But Willen shows what the economics can look like.

Anthropic's Claude helps with bookkeeping, customer service, advertising, and inventory across his businesses, while OpenAI image tools perform work that once went to photographers and designers.

Without AI, Willen said, "I'd probably need one other full-time person, plus I'd need to use contractors for photography, design, bookkeeping, and ad management."

A traditional product photography shoot could cost $2,000 to $5,000, and a designer $300 to $1,000 for new listing images, he said.

When AI directly eliminates one of those expenses, Willen said, "that money just goes into my pocket."

AI has also allowed him to pursue revenue channels he would not have previously considered. After acquiring Lone Trail, a hunting-gear brand selling binocular harnesses, packs, and other outdoor accessories, Willen said its Shopify site accounted for roughly 1% of sales. Rather than shut down the channel, he used ChatGPT and Claude to rebuild the site and AI to help run Meta advertising. In a recent 30-day period, he said, Shopify accounted for roughly 30% of Lone Trail's revenue.

"That's going to end up being at least six figures of annual revenue that would not exist without AI," Willen said.

Ginn's business shows how quickly a new entrant can reach meaningful revenue without first building an organization around it. She uses AI to help with proposals, pricing, strategy, and other work and is exploring an AI agent to perform part of a labor-intensive client process.

A survey conducted by accounting and invoicing software company FreshBooks identified similar behavior more broadly. Seventy-nine percent of solopreneurs said they had tried AI before hiring someone for a task they could not perform themselves (the same figure was 86% among when including microbusiness owners). AI allows them to "look much bigger than they are," FreshBooks CEO Shaheen Javadizadeh said.

But among existing small businesses, the available data tells another story. Gusto, a payroll and HR software company that serves small businesses, tracked businesses after they adopted AI and found their headcount was about 7% higher after a year than a comparable businesses that knew about AI but had not adopted it. Among firms with fewer than 10 employees, AI adopters grew headcount about 10% more than comparable non-adopters.

Willen's experience also highlights a tradeoff in the new business model: operating with fewer people does not eliminate reliance on larger technology platforms. Roughly 80% of its overall revenue still comes through Amazon, and he uses Meta and Shopify to expand direct sales. Ginn has no staff, but LinkedIn has been one of the biggest drivers of customers to her consultancy, she said.

Brynjolfsson said the proliferation of smaller producers can actually increase the value of the platforms that connect them with the buyers.

"There's this long tail of products, but at the same time, big platforms like Amazon are more powerful than ever because they aggregate all of these and they bring them together," Brynjolfsson said.

But the relationship is not as simple as labor savings flowing directly to Big Tech. Willen said some of the money he saves becomes profit, while Ginn spends less than $60 a month on paid AI tools.

AI can make it economical for more small producers to exist. And as core business production becomes cheaper, small businesses place greater focus on customer acquisition and distribution, Brynjolfsson said.

Brynjolfsson believes that while solopreneurs will thrive, use of AI will at the same time lead to the creation of even larger firms, on average, than exist today.

In a September research report titled "AI's Use of Knowledge in Society," Brynjolfsson and Zoë Hitzig, a researcher at the Anthropic Institute who studies the social and economic impacts of AI, argue that as AI codifies knowledge previously scattered among workers and enables more information to be aggregated and acted upon centrally, firms will grow larger on average, while industry concentration increases and local managerial autonomy declines. The researchers say early evidence is largely consistent with those patterns.

That's bad news for medium-sized companies. Brynjolfsson predicts AI could contribute to a "hollowing out" of midsize firms — very small teams capable of doing more at one end and giant organizations exploiting AI and scale at the other.

"The fat in the middle — the layers of management focused on internal coordination — will likely be significantly reduced," he said.

Adam Branch has experienced a version of that shift from inside two very different technology companies.

Before founding rhino.ai, an enterprise software company that uses AI to modernize legacy systems, Branch built Incentive Technology Group, a traditional technology services company that grew to roughly 500 employees. Under that model, winning more work meant hiring more people, he said.

"With rhino.ai, we're building AI software to do more of the work those teams used to do," said Branch.

Rhino.ai has roughly 50 employees. Branch estimates comparable modernization work can now be performed with three to four times the productivity per employee.

His team estimates labor represented roughly 85% of operating spending at ITG, and technology about 5%. At rhino.ai, Branch puts labor at roughly 40%, while technology, including cloud, software, and AI models, represents about 25%.

AWS sees a similar loosening of the relationship between revenue and headcount among AI-native startups. Its Engines of Growth research, an independent global study of 3,413 startup founders and senior leaders across 20 countries, found those companies grew revenue an average 156% annually versus 65% for startups overall, while more than half generated over $400,000 in revenue per employee. The report focused on companies under five years old that use AI in its most advanced forms.

"Growth used to be tied directly to how many people you could hire," said Ruba Borno, vice president of AWS Specialists and Partners. "Now founders can scale with technology, not just headcount."

Brynjolfsson does not see the result as simply Big Tech winning at everyone else's expense. Entrepreneurs can keep some of the productivity gains, consumers get more choice, and platforms benefit from supplying the technology and connecting more producers with customers, he said.

"The platforms and the people who are creating the tools are winning because they are able to sell them, the solopreneurs are winning because they are able to use them, and the consumers are winning because they are getting new products that they didn't get before," he said. "It's mostly a bigger pie, so it's a win-win-win — a triple win."

What AI may change is the shape of that bigger pie, he said.

"Whenever there's a technology change, a platform change, obviously it benefits the companies that are creating those platforms and technologies," Brynjolfsson said. "But it also benefits new entrants who can start fresh."

#2973. Observations after Sixers suffer blowout loss to Cavs, fall in NBA Cup

Observations after Sixers suffer blowout loss to Cavs, fall in NBA Cup originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers couldn't rekindle the magic of their comeback home-opening win over the Cavaliers.

They fell Friday night in NBA Cup action, suffering a 119-95 defeat in Cleveland. The Sixers dipped to 8-2 and the Cavs improved to 5-4.

The Sixers' top scorers were Jaylen Brown (20 points), VJ Edgecombe (19 points) and Tyrese Maxey (17).

The Cavs got 23 points from Donovan Mitchell and 22 from Evan Mobley.

The Sixers' injured players included center Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain). Cleveland was down big man Jarrett Allen (right knee contusion). Sam Merrill sat out the second half with a right elbow injury.

With Friday's loss, the Sixers are 1-1 in Eastern Conference Group B of the NBA Cup. Their next Cup game is scheduled for Nov. 25 against the Heat.

The Sixers will conclude their three-road trip Sunday afternoon against the Bulls. Here are observations on their blowout loss to Cleveland:

After a missed Merrill three-pointer, the Sixers steamed ahead on the fast break and Edgecombe opened the scoring with a slam.

The Cavs replied with a 15-0 run kickstarted by six straight Mitchell points. Much like in the Sixers' Oct. 22 win over Cleveland, they were many levels below their best in the first quarter.

The Sixers began 0 for 4 from three-point range, including two LeBron James misses. The Cavs were also the better team in transition and limited the Sixers' chances to attack in the open floor by taking excellent care of the ball in the early going. Edgecombe was called for his second foul with 7:34 left in the first quarter.

Meanwhile, Joel Embiid expressed his frustration about a couple of early no-calls. Embiid's larger problem was that he couldn't hit any sort of shot besides free throws.

He went 0 for 9 from the floor in the game. The seven-time All-Star finished with six points and seven rebounds.

Embiid has had a subpar shooting start to the year, especially from three-point territory — he's now 3 for 28 (10.7 percent) — but Friday was the kind of extreme night that's bound to happen here and there over the course of a long season.

Of course, it's notable that Embiid's been playing through right foot plantar fasciitis and the Sixers have been keeping a close eye on his minutes. We'll see how the team manages his availability moving forward and whether Embiid is able to bounce back next time out.

James didn't play anywhere near his finest game either. He had eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, eight rebounds, three assists and six turnovers.

As a team, the Sixers were 36.5 percent from the field and 25 percent beyond the arc.

Jabari Walker rejoined the Sixers' rotation as the backup center with Bona sidelined. Anfernee Simons, former Cav Dean Wade and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope all remained rotation fixtures.

With Walker and Caldwell-Pope on the court next to three starters, the Sixers cut their deficit to single digits in the second quarter. Walker threw down a fast-break dunk that cut Cleveland's lead to 48-41. He gave the Sixers some good defensive possessions when switched on to James Harden and provided his usual physical, high-effort work on the glass.

Edgecombe was fantastic in the second, sinking three triples, racking up 15 points and nabbing three steals.

Many second-year players would have a tough time finding a rhythm after encountering early foul trouble, but Edgecombe was great as soon as he checked back in and delivered a ton of valuable buckets in the Sixers' comeback push.

The Sixers pulled to within 66-63 when Maxey picked a steal and threw a pretty one-handed bounce pass to Edgecombe for a dunk.

With Embiid and James offering little as scorers, Brown hit several shots that helped the Sixers stay in the game. After his 30-point performance last time out against the Pistons, Brown reached the 20-point mark for a third consecutive game.

Still, the Sixers' offense never got rolling. The Cavs maintained their lead and extended it late in the third quarter. By early in the fourth, the Sixers trailed by over 20 points.

Embiid didn't play the fourth quarter and logged 25 minutes. He's had zero made field goals on just one other occasion in his career, back in Nov. 2019 against the Raptors.

Even with their tremendous first nine games, the Sixers knew they'd hit speed bumps throughout the year. They'll try to get back on track Sunday in Chicago.

#2971. Chris Mara tells The Post what’s changed with J.J. McCarthy — two years after Giants passed on QB in draft

Two years ago, the Giants had a chance to select a potential franchise quarterback in the top 10 of the NFL draft.

Instead of taking J.J. McCarthy, the Giants picked receiver Malik Nabers sixth overall before taking QB Jaxson Dart late in the first round.

But McCarthy, thanks to Dart's season-ending knee injury, ended up in New York after all this fall. Chris Mara, the Giants' senior vice president of player personnel and brother of team CEO John Mara, spoke exclusively with The Post's Steve Serby this week and explained what's changed about McCarthy since he entered the draft in 2024.

"We had him rated very high from a skill set. … We weren't quite sure about the maturity level, but we loved his skill set," Mara said. "He showed the ability to win, the ability to lead. Seeing him three years later, this is a completely different-looking person for me."

J.J. McCarthy looks for an open receiver during a Giants' Week 6 loss to the Saints at MetLife Stadium. Bill Kostroun for NY Post

McCarthy was selected 10th overall by the Vikings and missed what would have been his entire rookie season because of injury. When he did return in 2025, he had a roller-coaster season, finishing with a 6-4 record but completing just 57.3 percent of his passes with 11 touchdowns against 12 interceptions.

He fell to third-stringer duty behind newcomer Kyler Murray and veteran Carson Wentz this year, and after Dart's injury, the Giants swooped in to get McCarthy for a fifth-round pick.

In just three games, McCarthy looks more like the quarterback who helped lead Michigan to a national title during the 2023-24 campaign. He's upped his completion percentage to 63.3 and has a 95.2 passer rating, over 22 points higher than last year's mark.

J.J. McCarthy walks off the field after the Giants' Week 6 loss to the Saints. Robert Sabo for NY Post

Giants senior personnel consultant Chris Mara speaks after John Harbaugh's introductory press conference as the team's new head coach on Jan. 20, 2026 in East Rutherford, N.J. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

#2967. What we learned as Warriors' streak reaches seven games with OT win vs. Magic

What we learned as Warriors' streak reaches seven games with OT win vs. Magic originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – Winning six straight games is hard enough. Extending their win streak to seven straight looked like a walk in the park for the Warriors on Saturday night, until it wasn't.

The Warriors started off extremely strong, just to watch a 16-point lead fade away. Contenders need to rise to the challenge and overcome adversity, and that's what the Warriors did in a 126-119 overtime win over the Orlando Magic at Chase Center.

Beating the Magic gave the Warriors their first seven-game win streak without Jimmy Butler since January/February of their 2022 NBA championship season.

Steph Curry was incredible in the fourth quarter and overtime, as was rookie Yaxel Lendeborg.

The elder Curry scored a game-high 41 points on 13-of-19 shooting, going 5 of 10 from 3-point range and 10 of 10 at the free-throw line. Lendeborg added 23 points on 9-of-15 shooting and had eight rebounds.

A revitalized Strength In Numbers bench continued to be huge for the Warriors, outscoring the Magic 42 to 31.

There will be a lot to clean watching the film, but the Warriors will take a gritty win in the end.

Here are three takeaways from the Warriors improving to 7-3 on the season.

Winning time is where superstars are made. There's nobody in the building that doubts Curry in the clutch. But every hero needs a co-star alongside him. For Curry, that's a rookie wearing a cape.

The fourth quarter was won on the backs of a 38-year-old Curry and a 24-year-old Lendeborg in his 10th career game.

In the first three quarters, Lendeborg scored seven points and was a bit of an afterthought. Then in the fourth quarter, Lendeborg utilized his size and skill set to score nine huge points on a perfect 4-of-4 shooting. But this was another Steph Show for everyone to sit back and enjoy his 14 fourth-quarter points.

Between the two, Curry and Lendeborg combined to score 23 points in the fourth quarter on 8-of-9 shooting. Curry was on the bench when overtime began, coming in at the 3:48 mark with the Warriors ahead by two points. He and Lendeborg scored 12 of the Warriors' 16 points in overtime to claim victory.

Winning the margins is a major talking point of Warriors coach Steve Kerr to have success. His team needs to take care of the ball, focus on hitting singles instead of home runs and win the possession battle. The Warriors didn't execute those key parts of the game against a shorthanded Magic team that seemed to grab every loose ball.

Let's start with turnovers. As the first quarter crept to a close, the Warriors began to get loose with the ball. And the theme continued in the wrong ways for the home team. By halftime, even with the Warriors enjoying a 16-point lead, they already had given up 10 turnovers for 14 points.

As the Warriors' lead evaporated in the third quarter, they kept counting turnovers and were up to 14 for 18 points going into the fourth. But the Warriors only had one turnover between the fourth quarter and overtime, giving the ball away a total of 19 times for 26 points.

Another major area of concern was on the offensive glass. The Magic continued to keep possessions alive, and it finally caught up to the Warriors. While the Warriors came down with a lowly three offensive rebounds, the Magic secured a ridiculous 24, which helped lead to 33 second-chance points compared to the Warriors' two.

However he can, Gui Santos wants to help the Warriors win games. He was pushing through a left knee injury that held him back, and finally had to be told to take a seat. Santos missed the previous two games, both Warriors wins, before returning Saturday night.

He was back in the starting lineup, but couldn't give the Warriors much. At all.

Santos played just 11-plus minutes and didn't have much of an impact. Playing less than he usually would, Santos had more turnovers (two) than shots made (one). That's in a game where he has just one rebound and no assists.

Defensively, Santos didn't have much burst either. It's clear he's less than 100 percent, and the Warriors will need a much better version of him soon. Santos finished as a minus-4 with three points and didn't leave the bench in the fourth quarter or overtime.

#2966. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2964. Player Personnel Handbook implies that the NFL can delay (or deny) Brandon Aiyuk's reinstatement

Until very recently, people throughout the league believed that the procedure for returning from the reserve/left squad list was administrative, and essentially automatic.

The Brandon Aiyuk case shows that, in the NFL's view, it definitely is not.

The Collective Bargaining Agreement is silent on the topic of the reserve/left squad list. The relevant language regarding placement (and eventual reinstatement) from the reserve/left squad list appears in the NFL's Player Personnel Handbook.

In the year when the player is placed on reserve/left squad, he can't play for his current team or any other team. His season is basically over. The window for reinstatement opens the next year.

Here's the key language from the Player Personnel Handbook regarding reinstatement: "A player on Reserve/Left Squad must apply to the Commissioner for reinstatement to his club's Active List. No player on Reserve/Left Squad shall be reinstated in the same season in which he is placed on Reserve. A player applying for reinstatement in a season subsequent to the season in which he is placed on Reserve/Left Squad is prohibited from being reinstated in the last 30 days of the regular season or thereafter during the postseason, and no such player will be reinstated during the period between the trading deadline of a subsequent season and the 30-day deadline unless the club initiates the reinstatement request and the Commissioner approves it."

While the provision doesn't say it expressly, the language implies that the Commissioner has apparently unlimited discretion when it comes to approving the application to reinstate the player. In Aiyuk's case, the Commissioner has dragged his feet since October 27, setting the stage for an apparent effort by the 49ers to leverage Aiyuk's desire to be reinstated to his willingness to reimburse up to $13 million in previously-paid bonus money.

The NFL Players Association has remained silent throughout the process, and perhaps for good reason. The absence of any provision in the CBA regarding the mechanics of the reserve/left squad list arguably means that the situation falls within the NFL's "management rights" clause, which allows the various teams "to manage and direct their operations in any manner whatsoever, except as specifically limited by the provisions of this Agreement."

With no limitations in the CBA as to the operation of the reserve/left squad list (or reinstatement from it), the NFLPA may be without options.

Precedent becomes relevant to the overall analysis. How has the league handled past requests for reinstatement from the reserve/left squad list? If the league has previously not delayed reinstatement (for whatever reason), there's an argument to be made that it should not be delayed as to Aiyuk.

Regardless, it's becoming increasingly obvious that the NFL and the 49ers are working together to tie Aiyuk's reinstatement to his satisfaction of the debt that the 49ers believe Aiyuk has incurred through his prior default under his contract. It should be two separate issues, with the 49ers having the ability to pursue the recovery of bonus money from Aiyuk and Aiyuk having the ability to be reinstated. By tying the two together, the 49ers have found a way to condition Aiyuk's reinstatement on his willingness to wire a significant payment to the 49ers.

Can the NFL and the 49ers do that? For now, they are. The question is whether Aiyuk and/or the NFLPA can come up with a legal argument against the obvious effort to block Aiyuk's return until he returns millions to the 49ers.

If Aiyuk wants to be reinstated quickly, his best (and perhaps only) play will be to return millions to the 49ers, with the ensuing goal of earning it back as his career continues elsewhere.

#2962. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.

#2950. How Steph Curry made clutch NBA history in Warriors' overtime win against Magic

How Steph Curry made clutch NBA history in Warriors' overtime win against Magic originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

In the Warriors' 126-119 overtime win over the Orlando Magic on Saturday at Chase Center, Curry went a perfect 4-for-4 on go-ahead shots during the final two minutes of regulation and the extra period. It's the first time in the tracking era (start of the 1996-97 NBA season) a player has made at least four field goals without a miss under those circumstances in a single game (h/t @automaticnba/X).

And that's not even the whole story. Curry also made two free throws in the final minute of regulation, with the first also giving Golden State the lead at the time. Additionally, with 2:21 to go in the fourth quarter, Curry made a layup that tied the game at 100.

So, all in all, that's six different instances down the stretch when Curry made a shot to either tie the game or take the lead.

Oh, and it's probably worth mentioning Curry finished with 41 points on 13-of-19 shooting from the field, 5 of 10 from 3-point range and a perfect 10 of 10 from the free-throw line — not to mention six rebounds and six assists.

One more key tidbit, in case you forgot: Steph Curry is 38 years old.

Saturday's performance was Curry's 47th game with 40-plus points since he turned 30 years old, as well as his 16th such game since turning 35. He now leads all NBA players in both categories all-time.

Finally, another impressive benchmark for the road.

Curry joined Oscar Robertson and Russell Westbrook as only the third point guard in NBA history to record 25,000 points and 5,000 rebounds in his career (h/t @statmuse/X).

Just another night at work for Chef Curry as the Warriors won their seventh consecutive game.

#2949. Mississippi Senate: Colom increases lead over Hyde-Smith to nearly 2,500 votes

Democratic challenger Scott Colom expanded his narrow lead over Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate race on Friday afternoon.

As of 5:30 p.m. ET, Colom led Hyde-Smith by 2,498 votes, out of a total of 953,222 counted, with an estimated 97% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press.

That was 685 votes more than Colom's lead over Hyde-Smith when the day began.

Ty Pinkins, an independent candidate, had 20,028 votes, or 2.10% of the total.

The AP estimated that as of 4:45 p.m. ET, there were close to 30,000 votes that remained to be counted.

The Senate elections in Mississippi and Alaska are the only Senate races yet to be called by the AP. Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats from Republicans, meaning they will hold at least 54 seats out of the 100 in the Senate in January.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola is leading incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan by 637 votes, a margin of just 0.29 percentage points. Peltola has slightly more than 48% of the votes cast, while Sullivan has slightly less than that.

Pinkins has told media outlets that he realized after a poll in April that he could not win the race, but that he decided to stay in to draw votes from Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins, who was the 2024 Democratic nominee in the Mississippi Senate race against Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, said a number of Republicans had contacted him to say they planned to vote for him because they did not want to vote for Hyde-Smith.

A final result may not be available until next week because more ballots can still arrive and be counted.

Mississippi's election procedures do not have an automatic recount provision.

Hyde-Smith's campaign set the stage for a potential legal battle over ballots on Thursday by demanding in a letter to county commissioners and circuit clerks in all 82 Mississippi counties that they immediately separate all provisional ballots cast in the election from regular and absentee ballots.

"These measures are necessary to ensure that each affidavit ballot's eligibility for counting and ultimate disposition can be verified," the letter said.

Lawyers for Colom's campaign responded with a letter of their own to the counties, calling the Republican's demands "unlawful and extraordinary measures."

"Those demands have no basis in Mississippi law, and if you depart from ordinary election procedures as you have been asked to do, you will violate Mississippi voters' rights and Mississippi election law," they said.

Provisional ballots, which are also known as affidavit ballots, are cast by a voter whose name does not appear on the poll book, who does not present an acceptable photo ID, or in several other situations.

The ballot is then placed in a sealed envelope and later examined by election officials to see if it can be cast, or the voter can return with a photo ID or evidence within five business days to show they are entitled to cast the ballot.

"Absolutely, I didn't think it would be this close," said Brian Anderson, a political science professor at the Mississippi University for Women in Columbus.

Anderson said that before the election, he thought Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, might get into position to lose by just 3 or 4 percentage points, which would have been a very solid showing in a state that hasn't elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982.

"I'm much more surprised" now that Colom has a chance to win, Anderson said.

If Colom defeats Hyde-Smith, 67, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

"The Democrats have struggled here to create a pipeline of candidates," Anderson said. A Colom win "would galvanize the Democratic Party in a way it has not been," he said.

Neither candidate's campaign immediately responded to requests for comment from CNBC.

Hyde-Smith's X account, in a post Thursday afternoon, said, "Cindy Hyde-Smith is going to represent Mississippi in the United States Senate for the next six years."

"We believe thousands of votes remain to be counted from dozens of Republican counties, and we are confident those votes will reinforce her path to victory," the post said.

"Election officials are continuing the normal process of counting outstanding ballots, including absentee ballots. They must have the time to complete that work and ensure every lawful ballot is accurately counted and every Mississippian's voice is heard."

The post added, "We expect Democratic lawyers to pull out every stop, and we are prepared. Our focus is on completing the count and returning Cindy Hyde-Smith to the Senate to keep fighting for Mississippi values."

"Mississippi is the firewall, and this race is our line in the sand," the message said.

In a pair of ironic twists, if Colom wins, his victory could be attributed in some ways to actions taken by two Republicans: President Donald Trump and Hyde-Smith herself.

Supreme Court Justice Amy Coney Barrett, whom Trump appointed, wrote the court's 5-4 majority ruling in June that upheld a Mississippi law allowing mail-in absentee ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if they are received within five business days.

Those ballots could provide Colom a margin of victory. There are also an estimated 10,000 to 15,000 provisional ballots that require verification of eligibility to vote.

A lawyer for the Republican National Committee, whose lawsuit argued federal law barred counting votes received after Election Day, noted in a brief to the Supreme Court, "Democratic voters tend to mail their ballots later on average than Republican voters, which results in late-arriving ballots favoring Democratic candidates."

Trump at the time of the Supreme Court ruling called it "a tremendous loss" for voters' rights.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to her Senate seat in 2018 after the resignation of Thad Cochran and later that year won a special election for the seat. She was elected to a full term in 2020.

In 2023, she effectively blocked Colom's nomination by then-President Joe Biden to a post as a federal judge for the Northern District of Mississippi. Colom's nomination had the support of Mississippi's other Republican senator, Roger Wicker, and two former GOP governors.

Hyde-Smith said she opposed Colom's nomination for reasons that included his purported support for transgender students participating in girls' sports and because his campaign for district attorney had allegedly received money from billionaire George Soros.

Colom, in an April 10, 2023, letter to Hyde-Smith, said he was unaware before his hometown newspaper reported it that Soros had donated to a political action committee that had supported his campaign. He also wrote that he was not sure why Hyde-Smith had suggested he "opposed legislation to protect female athletes."

Colom concluded the letter by promising to be guided by the law, not his personal views on public policy, if he were confirmed as a judge.

"I hope you will reconsider your views about my nomination," he wrote.

Hyde-Smith did not reconsider her views and maintained her block on the nomination.

Colom announced his candidacy for the Democratic nomination for Senate in September 2025 and recently told CNN, "Me not having a federal judgeship is not a motivating factor for me at all" in running.

Anderson said Hyde-Smith ran "a low-visibility campaign," at least compared to Colom's, and that she focused on voters who traditionally would be expected to support her.

Hyde-Smith refused to debate Colom several times.

Colom "knows how to speak to people's interests, and Hyde-Smith was all about values, which is great for a lot of people, but it's all about the interests right now," Anderson said.

Those interests include concerns about affordability, the Iran war and corruption.

Colom is "bringing up all those," Anderson said.

Anderson noted that Colom's campaign had pounced on a remark Hyde-Smith made during a Feb. 22 interview with a local television outlet.

In the interview, Hyde-Smith answered a question about rising beef prices by saying, "You know, there so many proteins to choose from."

Colom's campaign featured a video clip of that remark in an ad featuring people reacting incredulously to Hyde-Smith's suggestion.

"Pick another protein?" one woman said in the ad. "She done lost her d--- mind. I'd rather pick another senator."

#2948. Jauan Jennings’ shocking decision to stay in Minnesota revealed

Last Sunday, NFL insider Jay Glazer revealed that the 49ers attempted to trade and reunite with Jauan Jennings but were denied.

On Saturday, the reason why Jennings decided to stay was revealed.

"I just thought it was best for both teams," Jennings told ESPN's Kevin Seifert. "No distractions. I love where I'm at."

Jauan Jennings wearing a San Francisco 49ers uniform in an NFL game against the Giants. Bill Kostroun/New York Post

Jennings spent the first five years of his career with the 49ers, where he went from being a long-shot seventh-round pick to a staple in their offense.

In 2024, Jennings was the team's second-leading receiver behind George Kittle, where he had a career-high 77 receptions with 975 yards and finished with six touchdowns.

Jennings saw a decrease in yards (643) and receptions (55) last season, while he went over his previous career-high touchdown mark by hauling in nine.

On Wednesday, Kyle Shanahan confirmed that the 49ers met with Jennings prior to Glazer's report, but the receiver chose to stay in Minnesota because he is happy.

"But he was like, 'Man, Kyle, I'm pretty happy. I'm liking stuff.' When I heard the way he talked and where we're at, I agreed with him. It was a good conversation and something that was possible, but that's why it didn't go down," Shanahan said.

San Francisco 49ers head coach Kyle Shanahan on the sideline during an NFL game against the Falcons. Getty Images

Over the offseason, he signed a one-year, $8 million contract with the Vikings to be their No. 3 receiver behind Justin Jefferson and Jordan Addison.

The Vikings (6-1) have a defense that's ranked second in sacks (25), but their offense ranks fourth worst in football, averaging 279.4 yards per game.

Part of the Vikings' offensive flaws are due to the inconsistency at quarterback. Kyler Murray has missed two starts and is set to miss Sunday's game against the Bills due to a hamstring injury, paving the way for Carson Wentz to get his third start of the season.

Jennings has been part of the damaged collateral in Minnesota's quarterback carousel, having only six receptions for 54 yards with no touchdowns this season.

#2945. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney
