#2944. Tech companies face mounting child safety lawsuits while pushing AI into schools

Tech companies have spent years telling parents they're working to make their products safer for children. But ongoing jury trials, settlements and related litigation against the likes of Meta, Google and Snap suggest that they're not doing enough.

The latest examples landed this week. Snap confidentially settled a case claiming that its technology caused severe psychological distress to two teenage users. Meta, TikTok and YouTube previously settled with the plaintiffs in the same suit. And Google was sued this week by the parents of a child who allegedly suffered a psychological crisis after chatting with the Gemini AI chatbot in Google Classroom.

Concern is intensifying as artificial intelligence companies pursue trillion-dollar valuations, and established internet giants pour money into developing advanced AI models while pushing them into the classroom. Products and features intended to maximize engagement can have detrimental effects on users, thus warranting "broader systemic changes in tech design, particularly when children are using them," said developmental psychologist Kate Blocker.

"I think it's so so important that children's safety is being considered when these products — AI, social media, et cetera — are being designed, and not after the fact as these problems emerge," said Blocker, the director of research and programs for the nonprofit Children and Screens.

Snap's settlement this week, which spared CEO Evan Spiegel from testifying in a Los Angeles court, continues a general trend of tech companies attempting to shield themselves from public scrutiny due to the various high-profile child-safety related trials this year, said Joseph McNally, the director of emerging litigation at McNicholas & McNicholas.

The case was one of several lawsuits related to the social media personal injury trial that Meta and YouTube lost earlier this year. More trials are slated to commence in March.

"I view this settlement and the other settlements in this space as an effort by tech companies to turn a corner, not only because I think that there were real legal challenges, but also the reputation of their products, the reputation of their companies has really been hit," said McNally, a former acting U.S. attorney for the Central District of California.

"The parties are pleased to have been able to resolve this matter in an amicable manner," a Snap spokesperson said in a statement.

But there remains a federal case involving Meta, TikTok, Snap and YouTube and a litany of nationwide school districts, along with various state attorneys general suits. In August, Meta agreed to pay nearly $17 billion to settle with a group of state AGs in a child mental health case. Some AGs are still pursuing similar suits against Meta's rivals.

The social media lawsuits have helped inform parents and the public about how the industry designs products used by teenagers and children, McNally said.

"It's the technology arms race that continues," he said. "What is oftentimes missing is how you balance that participation in the arms race with utility and then product safety."

Google ran into more criticism this week for moving too fast. The New York Times reported on Tuesday that K-12 schools that don't allow use of Google's AI tools for students will lose access to search. Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that, "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

That coincided with the new lawsuit against Google centering on a teen's alleged suicidal crisis that the parents attributed to the company's Gemini AI. The plaintiff's attorneys allege that "despite Gemini's known risks, Google hastily integrated" Gemini into Chromebook computers for school children.

"We are reviewing the claims in the lawsuit and we take this very seriously," a Google spokesperson said in a statement. "We've worked extensively with medical professionals and academic experts to build features that help young people find critical support and trusted resources when they need it."

Google and its internet peers have for decades been able to rely on Section 230.

Passed in 1996, the federal legislation has protected social media companies from being sued over content posted by their users. That's why plaintiffs' attorneys and state AGs have been focused on alleged harms stemming from the design of the tech platforms as opposed to their content.

Some plaintiffs are using the same tactics against AI companies. OpenAI faces several lawsuits from state AGs and plaintiff attorneys, taking cues from the social media suits in alleging that the design of ChatGPT can pose risks to children.

The litigation could become a growing financial risk as the company plans for a potential blockbuster IPO. OpenAI competitor Anthropic, which is planning to go public this month, highlighted the issue in its prospectus, even though the Claude creator prohibits children from using its product.

"This is a clear indication that tech companies anticipate more litigation risks than they did a decade ago," said Michele Neitz, a professor at the University of San Francisco School of Law.

Blocker said that the highly personalized nature of current AI-powered chat apps makes it "a little bit unpredictable" to determine exactly what the apps will say to kids. She stressed the importance of testing and evaluating these products for children's developmental safety from the start, "not after harms start happening."

This collective litigation has helped educate the public on the potential pitfalls of poor product design choices, Blocker said. And it could be a catalyzing force for change in the tech industry.

"Certainly there's a hope that this will motivate real systemic change, both from a regulation standpoint and internal company decision-making standpoint," Blocker said.

If you are having suicidal thoughts or are in distress, contact the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 for support and assistance from a trained counselor.

#2942. Chris Wilder Refuses to “Take a Wrecking Ball” to Sheffield United After Charlton Setback

Sheffield United suffered a frustrating 2-1 defeat away at Charlton Athletic on Saturday afternoon, missing the opportunity to climb into the Championship's top seven.

The Blades travelled to The Valley looking to extend their positive run of form after victories over Wrexham and Preston North End, but were unable to make it three consecutive league wins.

Charlton started brightly and looked determined to put the visitors under pressure from the opening whistle. Their positive approach was rewarded in the 12th minute when Alli found space on the edge of the penalty area before sending a low effort towards the near post. Michael Cooper was unable to keep the shot out, giving the hosts an early advantage.

The Addicks continued to threaten and almost doubled their lead shortly afterwards. Tyreece Campbell delivered a dangerous ball across the face of goal, but Mwamba was unable to make the necessary contact from close range as the opportunity went begging.

Sheffield United gradually grew into the contest and found an equaliser with 25 minutes on the clock. Romelle Donovan was brought down by Alli, allowing the visitors to deliver a dangerous free-kick into the Charlton penalty area.

Kalvin Phillips took responsibility for the set-piece, with his delivery causing considerable problems for goalkeeper Mannion. The Charlton stopper could only push the ball back into a dangerous area, allowing Jordan Ayew to react quickest and head into the unguarded net.

The Blades looked to build on their equaliser and came close to turning the game around moments later. Mannion again struggled to deal with a Phillips delivery, this time from a corner, but managed to recover in time to collect a header from Kitching before it crossed the line.

Chris Wilder made a change at the interval, introducing Femi Seriki in place of Hamza Choudhury. However, the defender's afternoon was brought to an abrupt end just two minutes into the second half.

Following a physical contest with Campbell, Seriki went down in considerable discomfort and required lengthy treatment before eventually being stretchered off. Mark McGuinness was brought on to replace him, with the incident proving a significant blow for the visitors.

With the game approaching its final stages, Charlton introduced Matty Godden for Kelman in the 75th minute as they searched for a decisive goal.

The substitution proved inspired. Just four minutes after coming onto the pitch, Godden found the net to restore Charlton's advantage.

Alli was once again at the heart of the action, driving towards the Sheffield United defence before lifting an inviting ball into the penalty area. Godden had found the perfect position and made no mistake, guiding a first-time finish beyond Cooper to send The Valley into celebration.

The Blades were unable to find another equaliser, suffering a disappointing defeat that leaves them frustrated heading into the international break.

Speaking to SUTV after the defeat, Wilder admitted that his side had missed an opportunity, while also expressing his disappointment with the manner of the two goals conceded.

The United boss felt his players had shown plenty of effort but acknowledged that they never managed to establish the control he wanted.

"Yeah, it's really disappointing. I think there was plenty of effort. It was a typical third game in a week in the Championship and it could have gone either way.

"I think we had the better of the chances and they were two really poor goals from our point of view. I think Michael [Cooper] will be a touch disappointed with the first one.

"We never really got control of the game as we would have wanted. They were fighting for their lives and their performance was a positive one, but it's a missed opportunity."

However, the biggest concern for Sheffield United supporters will undoubtedly be the injury suffered by Femi Seriki.

On the defender's condition, Wilder revealed: "He's out for the season. I think he's ruptured his patella. So yeah, it's a really, really disappointing one."

Sheffield United forward Jordan Ayew in action for the Blades away at Charlton Athletic. (Image Credit: Sheffield United Football Club).

Wilder acknowledged that the results recently have sometimes been difficult to make sense of, but insisted his focus remains firmly on improving his own team rather than worrying about the wider Championship picture.

"You look at the results after we came in and you scratch your head. But the one we have to concentrate on is our own and we have not done enough things right.

"We didn't capitalise on our moments to get the result we were all after."

Despite the frustration, the United boss was keen to avoid an overreaction, insisting that his side would not allow one disappointing afternoon to derail their recent progress.

"I'm not going to take a wrecking ball to it," he stated. "There is always emotion in it – when you win then you're going up and when you don't then it is a different narrative.

"We will keep calm, like I have all the way through. I kept calm after we beat Wrexham, Bristol City and when we won on Tuesday. There are mini setbacks, mini highs and mini lows that we need to deal with.

"It is a missed opportunity. I'd have loved to have climbed into the top seven, but we need to rest and recover over the international break."

The Blades now have an opportunity to rest and also work on tactics over this international break before facing Watford at home in a couple of weeks time.

#2941. TAKEAWAYS: What stood out from Nets young players in G League opener?

That is the name of the game for the Long Island Nets, who kicked off their season on Friday night against the Greensboro Swarm, the Charlotte Hornets' affiliate. While Long Island ultimately fell 121-111, there was plenty to take away, especially with some of Brooklyn's young players in the mix.

With first-round picks Drake Powell and Ben Saraf having played ample minutes alongside rookie Joshua Jefferson and two-way players Grant Nelson and Tyler Bilodeau, let's take a closer look at their performances from Friday night.

Saraf, now in his second year after being drafted in the first round of the 2025 NBA draft, didn't deliver the shooting performance you'd expect.

The 20-year-old finished with eight points on 3-of-14 shooting, including a rough 1-of-8 outing from beyond the arc. Entering this season, the biggest question surrounding Saraf's game was if he could find consistency from 3-point range.

Despite those struggles, however, he still managed to impact the game as a facilitator. The 6'6" 20-year-old guard dished out seven assists, which led the team. That's what makes him interesting. When his shot isn't falling, he can create chances for his teammates, but that also comes with turnovers, and he committed three in 32 minutes. But a 2.3-to-1 assist ration is seen as a very good number in the NBA.

His passing ability has been known. The next step in his development, though, is developing into at least a respectable scoring threat. The Nets' backcourt in Brooklyn is a logjam, especially with Nolan Traoré's strong play to open the season.

For Saraf to get his chance in the NBA this season, he'll need to be more than a facilitator.

The only other member of the Flatbush 5 who played on Friday was Powell. Like Saraf, the UNC product had a tough night on offense.

Powell tallied just six points on 2-of-6 shooting in 25 minutes, missing all three deep attempts. Despite the rough night, he managed four rebounds, three assists, a steal, and a block.

Similarly to Saraf's game, though, that was nothing new. Powell's defensive potential has been the most promising element of his game, given the length and athleticism he boasts with a 6'6″ frame and a 7-foot wingspan, ideal for a defender. He showed that facet of his profile again vs. Greensboro.

However, his offense remains a work-in-progress, at best. During Summer League, the 22nd pick in 2025 pick struggled mightily with his jump shot, shooting a brutal 1-for-28 from the field, including zero makes on 14 deep attempts.

If he can develop into a reliable catch-and-shoot option while becoming someone who can defend multiple positions at the NBA level, that's a valuable player. Coming away with a legitimate 3-and-D guy at the 22nd overall pick would be a really nice win for the Nets towards the end of the first round.

The issue, though, is that he is now in Year 2 and doesn't look particularly close to becoming that type of player. For someone with his defensive tools, even mild offensive improvements would make a big difference.

Obviously the upside is there, but Powell has to start putting the pieces together.

Perhaps the most exciting part from Friday was simply getting an extended look at Joshua Jefferson. After all, it's been a long time coming.

He was selected with the 28th pick in June's draft, a byproduct of the Julius Randle-for-Nic Claxton trade, and played minimally throughout Summer League as thst trade took until the eve of Summer League to be finalized. He couldn't even practice with his Nets teammates. Then, he missed Brooklyn's preseason due to a lower back injury.

Given all of that, Friday was the first real look that fans got on the Iowa State product, and he didn't disappoint, coming away with 15 points, six rebounds, and three assists, shooting 5-of-9 overall and 3-of-7 from three. Not a bad first impression.

Standing at 6'9" and 240 pounds, Jefferson stands out because he is very well-rounded and brings a little bit of everything to the table, hence his solid box score. There's always a role for someone like that in the NBA, especially with the size that he offers. It's why the term "point forward" was commonly used to describe him throughout the pre-draft process.

Given the abundance of wings up in Brooklyn, it's going to be tough for him to crack the NBA rotation right away, but Friday was a strong showing in the first-round rookie's first legitimate chance.

If there was one thing the Nets knew they were getting when they selected Bilodeau with the 43rd overall pick in June's draft, it was his shooting ability.

Throughout 64 games at UCLA in two seasons, he shot a whopping 43.8% from three-point range. It was on a significant sample size, too, attempting 233 threes as a Bruin. His 46.0% last season was the highest of any player taken in the 2026 Draft.

While it's just one game, that skill was on full display during Long Island's opener. The UCLA product finished with 13 points, four rebounds, two assists, and five steals, while shooting 5-of-10 from the field and 3-of-7 from deep.

Beyond the shooting, though, what jumps off the page is his five steals in 25 minutes. With that outing, he became the first Long Island Net in franchise history, dating back to 2016, to log five steals in a G League debut.

That matters because one of the biggest concerns about Bilodeau's game coming out of college was his defense. So seeing him make such a noticeable impact on that end right away matters…

Speaking with NetsDaily at Long Island's Media Day last week, he emphasized how much he feels his game has already grown since Summer League, adding that defense has been "a big improvement."

"I feel like I've improved so much since Summer League," the No. 43 pick said. "I think a big improvement of mine has been defensively, trusting myself a little more, and getting used to guarding the perimeter. I didn't do much of that last year."

Obviously, Bilodeau's top trait is shooting, and when he gets his opportunity in Brooklyn, it'll most likely be because of that calling card. But at 6'8" and 228 pounds, he certainly has the size to blossom into at least a serviceable defender, and Friday was a step in that direction for him.

Grant Nelson's start to the season on Friday is exactly what he needed to get off on the right foot. The 24-year-old finished with 18 points and 13 rebounds, including six offensive boards while shooting 8-of-16 from the field in 27 minutes.

He missed all five attempts from beyond the arc, which isn't ideal, but it was promising to see strong overall scoring output and activity on the glass as well as a willingness to take the deep shot. The Alabama product also used his size and athleticism on defense, coming away with a block and a steal.

The shots didn't go his way, especially from deep, but that's an area of his game Nelson spent a lot of time developing during his rehabilitation after patellar tendinitis cut his season short last year.

"I think it's extremely important with the direction that the game has been going in," he told us at Media Day. "I mean, I think it's just adding another part to my game, being versatile in that way. Most bigs can shoot now. So being able to do that, I think it will definitely help me out in my career."

At 7'0", Nelson is fascinating because he offers the exact tools that the Nets could potentially need in Brooklyn. With his athleticism, he brings a little bit of everything, but most notably rim protection ability, which Long Island's parent club currently lacks.

Proving that he deserves an NBA chance, though, especially on a crowded roster, starts with taking advantage of his chances with Long Island, which Friday was an example of. Now he must build on that.

Long Island is back in action Saturday night against the Swarm, then faces the College Park Skyhawks, the Hawks affiliate on Tuesday, before their home opener Friday against College Park at the Nassau Coliseum.

#2940. Broncos and Bo Nix have won 16 straight one-score games

The Broncos keep finding ways to win games — especially close games.

Denver has won 16 straight games decided by one score or less. Quarterback Bo Nix has been the starter in each of the games.

Via NBC Sports research, the streak ties the 2023-24 Chiefs (with starting quarterback Patrick Mahomes) for the longest streak since 1950.

The Broncos last lost a one-score game in Week 3 of the 2025 season, 23-20 at the Chargers. Since then, the Broncos have won every one-score game in which they have played.

This year, they're doing it despite being routinely outgained. For the season, they have a yardage differential of minus-658 — third worst in the NFL. And the Broncos have won three games in 2026 despite being outgained by 100 or more yards.

The record for any season is four wins, accomplished by eight different teams. With two more this year, the Broncos will have the record to themselves.

The next opportunity to win a one-score game comes on Sunday, at the Panthers in a rematch of Super Bowl 50.

#2939. Yaxel Lendeborg Makes Heartfelt Admission About Buying His Mother a House Amid Cancer Battle

Yaxel Lendeborg reached an emotional personal milestone this week. The Golden State Warriors star rookie surprised his mother, Yissel Raposo, with a brand-new house, a moment he shared on his YouTube debut. As it turns out, it was no impulse purchase after gaining a 4-year, $28 million contract. He just fulfilled a dream he had harbored long before his name was ever called in the NBA Draft.

Following the Warriors' thrilling 126–119 overtime victory against the Orlando Magic on Saturday night, the 24-year-old forward reflected on the deep-seated motivation behind the life-changing gift.

When asked after the game when the thought of buying his mother a house first entered his mind, Lendeborg traced the desire back to his high school days, when he was working everyday jobs while watching others achieve similar feats online.

"When I was working day-to-day jobs in high school, I see people on YouTube doing that stuff with their mom and it's like, 'Man, this would be awesome,'" Lendeborg admitted postgame per an X post by KNBR. "Our house back home wasn't really like, it wasn't that bad growing up, but it's like now that when I got taller, when I got older, I'm like, 'Man, this house isn't like suitable for all six of us,'? So it was like, I got to work as hard as I can to get this done for my mom. So it took a little while to actually get in a position to do that, but I mean, since high school, honestly, it's been like something I've always wanted to do."

Lendeborg documented the emotional reveal in a YouTube video titled "This one's for Mama Yax," which captured Raposo being led to the front entrance before hearing her son say, "Welcome home." Overcome with emotion, Raposo fought back tears as she walked through the doorway of the house, a process Lendeborg revealed took two to three months to finalize.

"There is no gesture big enough to properly say thank you for all the love, sacrifice, and support my mom has poured into me, but this moment was pretty special and one I'll never forget," Lendeborg shared in a statement accompanied by the video. "She deserves it all. For me, it's bigger than basketball. It's about making sure the people who believed in me get to enjoy the blessings too."

The emotional moment carries profound weight given the extraordinary sacrifices Raposo made to guide her son's basketball journey. As detailed in a Players' Tribune essay earlier this year, Raposo, a former athlete herself, helped rescue Lendeborg's basketball future.

Back in high school, Yaxel went through a tough time after losing his grandfather. Moreover, his falling grades made him academically ineligible for athletics, with the possibility of not graduating with his peers.

Working 12-hour warehouse shifts and driving for Uber on weekends, Yissel mapped out an academic recovery plan, hounded college coaches, and leveraged connections with the Dominican national team to get him into a New York showcase.

That persistence launched Lendeborg's path from Arizona Western Junior College to UAB, and ultimately to Michigan, where he led the Wolverines to an NCAA national championship and earned Big Ten Player of the Year honors before the Warriors selected him 11th overall in the 2026 NBA Draft.

Making the gesture even more poignant is Raposo's ongoing health battle. In late 2025, she was diagnosed with Stage 4 appendix cancer. She has gone through at least nine rounds of chemotherapy but has been a visible presence at every one of her son's milestones.

"She's the strongest person I've ever known," Lendeborg wrote in The Players' Tribune. "She is my guardian angel. My hero."

The gesture resonated deeply across the Warriors organization. Head coach Steve Kerr praised the rookie before Saturday's game against Orlando.

"I saw it this morning. It's incredible," Kerr told reporters. "It is such a heartwarming moment, and it is life-changing for these guys to make the NBA, and life-changing for themselves and their families… We always love to see someone like Yaxel, who's taking care of his mom, taking care of his family. It's beautiful."

On the court, Lendeborg has backed up his off-court success with an explosive start to his professional career. He is averaging 21.0 points, 7.1 rebounds, and 4.1 assists per game, earning early favor in the NBA Rookie of the Year race.

On Saturday night, he chipped in 23 points and eight rebounds, including a critical 3-pointer in overtime, alongside Stephen Curry's 41 points to help Golden State secure a 126–119 win over the Magic, extending their winning streak to seven games.

For Lendeborg, however, securing a safe, comfortable sanctuary for the woman who saved his life remains the ultimate victory.

#2938. Jets’ Joseph Ossai ‘extremely’ frustrated he will miss fourth straight game

KANSAS CITY, Mo. — This was not how Joseph Ossai expected his first season with the Jets to go.

The edge rusher signed a three-year, $36 million contract with the Jets in March. So far, he has played only three games for the team.

Ossai will miss his fourth straight game with an ankle injury on Sunday after missing the first two games of the season with a foot injury. The Jets ruled Ossai out Saturday after he was limited in practice this week.

"Extremely [frustrating]," Ossai said this week about this season. "But I was listening to a podcast yesterday talking about the process. You know, injuries happen in football. It's been terrible for me, but they happen. When they do happen, you have to go through the process of getting back. That's what I'm focusing on right now, and when I get back I'll be ready to go."

The Jets hoped Ossai could provide a boost for their pass rush. Instead, he has barely seen the field. He has zero sacks and nine tackles this season. Ossai, 26, said he is trying not to get down as he misses games.

Joseph Ossai takes a knee before the Jets' Week 5 win over the Browns on Oct. 11, 2025 at MetLife Stadium. Getty Images

"I'm just focused on working through the process, one step at a time," Ossai said. "Just attacking what I have to do daily. Look at the smaller picture, not the big picture and then just go from there."

The Chiefs offense has been one of the best in the NFL over the past decade, and that unit now has another challenging dimension with the addition of running back Kenneth Walker III. The reigning Super Bowl MVP joined the Chiefs as a free agent this offseason, and he has rushed for more than 100 yards four times already. Walker is third in the NFL with 770 rushing yards.

The Jets defense knows it faces a tough challenge in stopping Walker.

Seahawks defenders tackle running back Kenneth Walker III during their win over the Chiefs on Oct. 25, 2026 at Lumen Field in Seattle. Getty Images

"When you're able to add an elite running back like Walker to an already explosive offense, it presents another dynamic that's very challenging to go against," Jets linebacker Demario Davis said. "For years, [Patrick] Mahomes and [Travis] Kelce have been the top tight end-quarterback tandem in the National Football League, and now they've added a running game on top of that.''

The Jets are 21st in rush defense. They were better last week, allowing 95 rushing yards to the Raiders after giving up 156 and 148 in their two previous games.

In addition to ruling out Ossai on Saturday, the Jets also ruled out CB Nahshon Wright S VJ Payne, who were both listed as questionable on Friday. … The Jets elevated LB Troy Reeder and OT Brant Banks from the practice squad for Sunday's game. … The NFL fined Raiders S Jeremy Chinn $17,911 for his hit on Jets WR Garrett Wilson at the end of last week's game. Wilson was concussed on the play. … Jets RG Joe Tippmann was fined $12,537 for a low block.

#2937. Is there a a €65 million problem waiting around the corner for Bayern Munich?

Bayern Munich might have a €65 million Dayot Upamecano problem, and nobody seems particularly interested in talking about it.

For months, one of the biggest unspoken questions surrounding Bayern Munich has been whether Dayot Upamecano's new contract would come with a relatively modest release clause that could make him vulnerable to an exit next summer. Now, the reported €65 million figure is back in the spotlight given how excellent the French center-back has been, and Bayern Munich board member for sport Max Eberl was given an opportunity to address it. Understandably, Eberl chose not to.

When asked about the rumored release clause, the Bayern sporting director offered a carefully worded response. "I'm not here to discuss the details of the contract. One thing is clear: we've extended Upamecano's contract, and we're absolutely delighted that we did. To be honest, he feels extremely comfortable here," Eberl told Tz's newsletter, Absolut Bayern (as captured by @iMiaSanMia).

That is certainly a nice sentiment, but it does not exactly answer the question.

Nobody at Bayern Munich wants to entertain the possibility of losing Upamecano, particularly with the French international establishing himself as one of the best center-backs in world football. However, if the reported €65 million release clause is real, the club could have very little control over his future next summer if a major European club decides to activate it.

Bayern Munich deserves credit for securing Upamecano's extension, but the circumstances surrounding that deal remain an elephant in the room. Everyone seems happy to celebrate the renewal, and nobody seems particularly eager to discuss what happens if someone comes calling with €65 million.

Hopefully, Upamecano does, indeed, enjoy playing with Bayern Munich and chooses to ignore any inquiries should they arrive.

#2936. What midterm election blue wave rebuke of Trump means for renewable energy

As recently as 2022, many leaders in corporate America and the investment community were singing the praises of ESG — environmental, social, governance — as the foundation of stakeholder capitalism. It didn't take long for that tune to change, for understandable reasons.

President Trump didn't just make "ESG", "sustainability" and "climate change" dirty words in his "green new scam" rhetoric. The Trump administration's rollback of environmental regulations, elimination of clean energy subsidies and tax credits, defunding and halting of wind and solar projects, and embrace of the fossil fuels industry accelerated the backlash.

But renewable energy investing remains alive and well, notably among public and private funders of infrastructure projects and clean technology innovation. And following the midterm elections that gave climate-friendly Democrats control of the House and Senate, the prospects of reviving the clean energy agenda suddenly brightened.

Clean energy and solar stocks rallied the day after the election results, which "potentially reduces the risk of further political interference with renewable energy incentives and provides greater protection for the long-term economics of U.S. wind, solar and battery storage investments," Barclays analysts led by Peter Crampton wrote in a note.

While Trump can veto legislation to restore subsidies and credits and revive projects, and continue to issue executive orders on energy policy, some Republicans may be more inclined to help Democrats override the president in order to appeal to voters outraged over high energy prices — a sentiment expressed in the passage of multiple state initiatives to impede the build-out of electricity-hungry data centers.

The blue wave also shines a spotlight on a bipartisan permitting-reform bill awaiting Senate approval. Several progressive Democrats want to put off the vote until the new Congress convenes in January, giving them time to rewrite provisions favoring renewables projects over fossil fuel ventures.

Ultimately, the midterms could improve the outlook for investors focused on clean energy. That is likely to start with impact investors who specifically seek to generate a competitive financial rate of return alongside positive, proactive social and environmental impact. Opportunities can be found through public equity investments like mutual funds and ETFs that are constructed to invest in clean energy companies. But many investors also seek out pure-play clean energy positions offered through fixed income, private equity, private credit, venture capital, real assets and community-focused investments.

"There are many different approaches that could lead impact investors to be thinking about clean energy investments," said Amit Bouri, who's retiring as CEO of the Global Impact Investing Network at year's end. GIIN's 2026 survey of impact investors found that 56% have funds invested in clean energy and 25% plan to increase those investments over the next five years. The survey was conducted prior to the midterms.

"Clean energy infrastructure can be really compelling from an investment standpoint, because the financial thesis and impact thesis are mutually reinforcing," said Justina Lai, chief impact officer at LNW, a wealth management advisory firm. LNW has about $3.5 billion of its total $12.1 billion assets under management invested in impact strategies. She added that it's challenging to break down the number of clients who are specifically focused on clean energy and clean tech. "Climate change or climate action writ large is likely the most common impact priority that we encounter with our clients," she said.

While it might be hard to focus on the potential in the renewable energy sector in 2026 with energy commodities leading all sectors of the market as crude oil prices surged, some of the factors Lai cites should be on the radar of investors beyond the impact investing space.

She pointed to the nation's growing electricity demand — partly tied to data centers — its aging and constrained grid infrastructure, the increasing need for energy storage, the effects of geopolitics on energy security and rising energy costs as major factors driving clean energy investing. Simultaneously, though, because of declining costs and continuing maturity of many clean technologies, "we know that renewables are the lowest cost form of new-build generation, even on a fully unsubsidized basis," she said.

The proof is in the gigawatts. The U.S. is on pace to add a record 89 GW of clean energy capacity by the end of 2026, with installations projected to exceed that level in 2027, according to BloombergNEF's latest US Clean Energy Market Outlook. In just the first half of this year, the Environmental Defense Fund and Atlas Public Policy recently reported, 11.5 GW of solar, 8.3 GW of battery storage and 6.3 GW of wind power were installed.

U.S. investment in clean energy and transportation alone totaled $75 billion in the second quarter of 2026, the second-highest quarterly figure on record and up 22% from the previous quarter, according to the Clean Investment Monitor, a joint project of Rhodium Group and MIT's Center for Energy and Environmental Policy Research.

The supply chain has amped up, too. By the third quarter of this year, manufacturing capacity for solar modules, wind blades and nacelles had expanded by roughly 50% from the first quarter of 2025, reflecting new facilities that have come online, the CIM reported in October. And over the past four quarters, $277 billion was invested across the country in the manufacture and deployment of renewable energy, non-fossil fuel vehicles, building electrification and carbon-management technology.

"Investment has consistently been very strong in solar and storage at the utility scale," said Hannah Hess, director, energy and climate at Rhodium. That makes sense from a bottom-line perspective, she said, "particularly when some of the deadlines for these projects have changed for them to be eligible for tax credits."

Hess is referring to provisions in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which significantly hastened the sunsets and deadlines for federal clean energy tax credits compared to the original timeline set by the Biden administration's Inflation Reduction Act. Solar and wind projects had to begin construction by July 4 of this year or be placed into service by December 31, 2027, to claim standard investment or production tax credits.

Meanwhile, even with the Trump administration's concerted efforts to rescind much of the $1.6 trillion set aside for clean energy under Biden's Bipartisan Infrastructure Law and the IRA, nearly $600 billion remains available, according to a Politico analysis. Keeping that third of the funding pipeline open bodes well for clean energy developers and investors, especially considering the midterm results.

"Despite the gutting of the IRA, our long-term thesis and our clients' long-term views on clean energy remain the same," said Roraj Pradhananga, chief investment officer at Veris Wealth Partners, an advisory firm that specializes in impact investing. "If we look at the volatility and risk-adjusted returns of the traditional energy sector, in the long run, shifting to renewable energy makes the most [economic] sense," even when taking the tax credits out of the equation, he said.

Veris manages $3 billion across asset classes, Pradhananga said, using various vehicles, including public markets, private debt and infrastructure funds. "Climate solutions tend to be the largest allocation for our clients. We recently allocated to strategies from GPs like Closed Loop Partners, Streetlife Ventures, SJF Ventures, New Energy Capital and Sunwealth."

The proliferation of AI data centers was a hot-button issue in the midterms. Voters in 10 states weighed in on 41 local initiatives, which included complete bans on new construction, pausing ongoing and planned facilities and restrictions on energy consumption. All but one passed. And although newly elected governors in three states — Ohio, Texas and Georgia — ran on platforms critical of data centers, they're not calling for permanent bans.

For instance, Gina Hinojosa, governor-elect of Texas, said during a CNN interview on November 5, "We will have laws to protect Texans, and we need to stop what we are doing with data centers until we have those protections." She also emphasized giving local communities a meaningful say in whether and how facilities operate. That echoes Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro's cautious approach to data center development in that state. In August, he issued an executive order that places a host of restrictions on developers.

The upshot for AI companies may be a slowdown in new data center construction, increased input from local residents and officials, and self-financing for electricity and water usage. Yet they are not expected to change their long-term expansion plans. A few days before the election, Goldman Sachs said, "While U.S. data center development faces increasing political and community opposition, project data and our updated capacity estimates suggest that the...growth outlook through 2027 remains largely unchanged." The Wall Street firm forecast data center power demand to grow 38% or 12 GW in 2026 and 38% or 17 GW in 2027.

On Friday, OpenAI's CFO told CNBC the company will not be changing its investment plans despite the election results. White House economic advisor Kevin Hassett expressed a similarly optimistic view of continued investing on CNBC on Friday. "We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

From another perspective, the risks associated with AI and data centers could be beneficial to renewable energy investments. "If anything, sustainability concerns around AI actually reinforce their interest in climate action," Lai said. "The growth in AI and data centers could drive significant new fossil fuel generation, so our clients are motivated to ensure that more of that [electricity] demand is met by renewables."

That's true for many of Veris' clients, too, Pradhananga said. But he also acknowledged that because natural gas is a prominent fuel source for large-scale utilities, as well as behind-the-meter LNG turbines that power many data centers, "there are some [of our] clients who view this as part of the transition" from fossil fuels to renewables, espousing the often-mentioned "all of the above" approach to the changeover. "This is where we do have exposure to utilities in [our] public markets portfolio."

Public consternation over data centers, a Democratic-controlled Congress come January and, at least for now, a voting map across the country that has swung significantly towards the Democratic Party ahead of the 2028 presidential election, could create a trifecta of tailwinds for investors in renewable energy and clean-tech. And while any moratorium on data centers could impact renewable project development on a case-by-case basis, it might also promote investments in transmission grid upgrades that allow utility-scale solar and wind installations to tie into existing and proposed data centers.

It could also speed up research and development of emissions-free power generation, energy-efficiency and other technologies that climate-tech VCs are funding. Due to the AI boom, renewable energy companies have faced stiffer competition in recent years for venture capital dollars previously targeting climate change and decarbonization. But climate-tech VC hit $26 billion in the first half of 2026, a 55% increase year over year, according to market-intelligence firm Currence.

"There are very interesting infrastructure opportunities in [climate investing], including advanced fusion ventures, floating offshore wind turbines and solar plus storage operations," said Adam Wolfensohn, a clean-energy investment advocate and advisor. "All of these technologies keep getting better and cheaper, unlocking so much more potential for renewables and new financial structures. It's a rich time to be looking at this space."

Among the guiding resources the GIIN offers to its 350 member organizations in 40-plus countries, including 126 in the U.S., is a recently launched climate solutions investing framework. "It's designed to help investors make sense of the landscape of climate solutions and to ensure they have rigor around the actual climate impacts underpinning an investment strategy," Bouri said. "A lot of things have green labels on them, but what we are really focused on is what drives real results for people and the planet."

#2935. Commanders Roster Moves: Jer'Zhan Newton activated, Dyami Brown to IR

#2934. Sergio Garcia reveals what Luke Donald has already told him about the Ryder Cup ahead of Adare Manor

Sergio Garcia is on the outside looking in when it comes to the European Ryder Cup set-up right now.

The complete opposite is true for Rory McIlroy, who has played a key role for Team Europe in every event since 2010.

And the golfing legacies of the Spaniard and the Northern Irishman have headed in completely different directions over the past few years.

Garcia and McIlroy actually enjoyed many Ryder Cups together, notably the incredible win at Medinah in 2012 and the dominant wins in 2014 and 2018.

Luke Donald is someone who the Spaniard was very close with as well before he joined LIV Golf in 2022.

However, Garcia has not made the European Ryder Cup team since 2021, mostly due to the fact that he left the PGA Tour and DP World Tour to join LIV Golf back in 2022

The 46-year-old from Castellon is currently the highest points scorer in Ryder Cup history, and he will be desperate to add to his tally next year at Adare Manor.

With Garcia keen to rejoin the DP World Tour after his exit from LIV Golf, he may well be able to play his way back onto the Ryder Cup team.

During an interview with Golf Magic, Garcia was asked whether he has spoken to Donald about his chances of making the team at Adare Manor next year.

"I had a little chat with Luke in Ireland, mentioning that I was trying as hard as I could to make the team, and he said that he didn't expect anything else, so that was nice," revealed Garcia.

"I also told him that obviously if I couldn't be there as a player that I would love to help in any way possible."

Sergio Garcia was also asked whether he has spoken to any of the European players about a possible reintegration into the Ryder Cup setup.

"I haven't really talked to Rory [McIlroy] about it much, but I have talked to Justin Rose and a couple of others, and obviously they are on the same page," Garcia replied.

"If there is any way that I can help then they are happy for me to be there and that's what I'm going to try to do. So we'll see what the year brings us."

The onus is now on Sergio Garcia to play some exceptional golf over the next nine months or so in order to qualify automatically for the Ryder Cup team at Adare Manor.

However, if he is unable to qualify, it's highly unlikely that he will receive a captain's pick, given how badly he has damaged some of his relationships within the game.

#2933. Will this week's blue wave election usher in a Democratic president in 2028?

In surfer slang, a wave that lasts a long time is a "runner." A key question for investors is whether the blue wave that washed over the country Tuesday was that kind of wave, and could it ferry Democrats to victory in 2028.

Statistically, the answer is it should have little effect.

CNBC analyzed elections since 1938 and looked at the biggest midterm wave elections where the party in the White House lost seats to determine whether they portend anything about the presidential election two years later.

Tuesday's election was the biggest House seat loss for the incumbent party in the White House since 2018, but it only barely makes the historical Top 10. It ranks 10th in CNBC's analysis using an estimate of 41 House seats gained by the opposition party as close races are still being tallied. The largest loss came in 1938 when Democrats lost 72 House seats amid a renewed recession, opposition to court-packing by President Franklin Delano Roosevelt and the president's battle with conservatives in his own party. Roosevelt went on to win reelection two years later in 1940.

In more modern times, Republicans swept to power in the House in 2010 two years into Barack Obama's first presidential term, with Democrats losing 64 seats, though retaining control of the Senate. Obama went on to reelection two years later. Of the top four wave elections, which all occurred before a president's final term, the incumbent went on to win reelection each time.

But for the next five waves, the incumbent party lost, including John F. Kennedy beating Richard Nixon in 1960 after Republicans lost seats under President Dwight Eisenhower in 1958; and Joe Biden beating Donald Trump in 2020 after Republicans lost seats in 2018.

Jay Campbell, partner at Hart Associates, the Democratic pollster for the CNBC All-America Economic Survey, said the reason midterm waves may not carry forward is the importance of individual characters in presidential elections.

"A lot can happen in two years. And while presidential elections are certainly subject to the overall political environment, they are also very much about each party's nominee," Campbell said. "If candidate quality matters in congressional elections, it matters 1,000 times more in presidential ones."

It's a long way to 2028 and this wave, gnarly as it may have been, could statistically peter out just as easily as it could wash Democrats back into the White House.

#2932. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2931. J.J. McCarthy gets latest Giants road test in Philadelphia house of horrors

PHILADELPHIA — Life on the road is usually not easy. It has never really bothered J.J. McCarthy.

He listed Ohio State, Penn State and Michigan State as the most hostile places he ever visited as the Michigan quarterback, calling them "really the big three.'' Then he narrowed down that list to single out the most challenging road venue.

"I'd have to say The Horseshoe, for sure,'' McCarthy he said.

McCarthy started one game at Ohio Stadium in Columbus and beat the Buckeyes 45-23 during the 2022 season. He tossed three touchdown passes. McCarthy, in his first start for the Giants, won in Houston, beating the Texans 27-21 in overtime.

Next, the Giants play the Eagles on Sunday at Lincoln Financial Field, where they have not won since 2013. Consider it the most difficult environment for the Giants to deal with on the road.

"He's been in huge games,'' coach John Harbaugh said of McCarthy. "He's been in the biggest games in front of the biggest crowds in college football. And he's also done not just that, he did it also in the NFL too. So yeah, he's been there before. That's not going to be an issue. The issue is going to be the Eagles, and the issue is going to be us, how we operate, how we play, how we execute. That's really the thing he's going to be concerning himself with. The exterior stuff is not going to be a factor.''

J.J. McCarthy throws a pass during the Giants' Week 7 win over the Texans on Oct. 25, 2026 in Houston. Getty Images

McCarthy in 2025 started and won games for the Vikings in Chicago, Detroit and Dallas. He did not sound worried about the noise and venom at the Linc.

"It's all about us and how we execute the football plays,'' he said. "Obviously, it gets very loud, but the preparation throughout the week, the nonverbal communication is just a huge key in any environment, but especially on the road in loud crowd environments. But at the end of the day, it's just about executing those ball plays and not worrying about anything else.''

RB Saquon Barkley will play despite hamstring and back issues that forced him to miss two games and limited his snaps the past two games. He has yet to gain more than 83 yards in a game this season but is averaging 4.9 yards on his 56 rushing attempts.

"Oh, he's an elusive dude, man,'' Harbaugh said. "I mean, they're one of the league leaders in making tacklers miss. I'm pretty sure he's a big part of that, and that's just what he does.

"He's a threat catching the ball out of the backfield, checkdowns, things like that. So, all hands on deck when he's out there.''

The demise of the Eagles (2-6) includes a defense that has not lived up to its advance billing. They are 27th in total defense, 30th against the run, and 28th against the pass and 27th in scoring defense. Still, there are formidable players at every level, including Jalen Carter and Jordan Davis up front and Quinyon Mitchell and Cooper DeJean on the back end.

"Still a really good Vic Fangio defense,'' guard Jon Runyan Jr. told The Possaidt. "They have really good players everywhere. I'm not gonna comment on what's going on over there. Some people will say they're underperforming, but they're a really talented defense. A lot of the same exact guys they had the last couple of years are still around, very young and talented.''

The Giants made a series of moves Saturday.

They added three players for Sunday's game from their practice squad: WR Jalin Hyatt, RB Phil Mafah and OLB Dawuane Smoot. They also re-signed QB Jake Haener to the practice squad.

#2930. Trump says U.S. carried out ‘minor strikes’ on Iran as missiles hit Qeshm Island

President Donald Trump told reporters Friday that the U.S. carried out "minor strikes" on Iran, amid reports that two missiles struck Qeshm Island near the Strait of Hormuz.

The missiles struck the island on Friday, Iranian news agency Tasnim reported, according to Reuters. Tasnim said there was no official information on damage, Reuters added.

The island lies off Iran's southern coast, near the Strait of Hormuz, the vital artery for oil and gas that has become a flashpoint in the war.

Speaking to reporters Friday evening after landing in West Palm Beach, Florida, Trump didn't comment specifically on Qeshm Island, saying: "I don't want to talk about it."

But he added: "We do minor strikes, but they knock out. They built a little bit of an anti-aircraft device, and we knocked it out in about 15 minutes, maybe five." He did not explicitly confirm that he was referring to the reported strikes on Qeshm.

The strikes are the first since Tuesday's midterm elections, after analysts and regional officials had debated whether the vote would lead Trump toward further escalation or renewed diplomacy.

The U.S. struck the same island Monday, in what a U.S. official told MS Now was a "defensive measure."

Trump also told reporters Friday that Iran wants "to make a deal very badly. They have 300% inflation. Their navy's gone. Their airport, everything's gone, and they want to make a deal."

The International Monetary Fund forecast that Iran's consumer prices would rise by an average of 78.6% in 2026, in a report published Oct. 13.

When asked if he wanted to make a deal "really soon," Trump said: "Well, we have nobody getting hurt, nobody getting killed.

"You know, this is a war that we may have been there for seven months, but we probably fought for three or four weeks, really fought. And again, you look at World War One; that's a war. World War Two; that's a war."

Iran has said it wants more than "paper commitments" from the U.S. before entering a new phase of diplomatic talks.

"We must see practical measures by the Americans," Ali Bagheri Kani, deputy secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, told state broadcaster IRIB on Friday, according to Press TV.

#2929. 91-Year-Old Athlete, Who Started Competing in Track and Field at 60, Is Still Breaking World Records: ‘Never Too Late’

Florence Meiler started her track-and-field career 31 years ago

Now 91, the Vermont athlete is breaking world records, including in the 2,000-meter steeplechase, 80-meter hurdles, pole vault, high jump, triple jump and pentathlon

During motivational speeches, Meiler tells fellow senior citizens that it's "never too late" to take up a sport or try new things

Three decades ago, a then-60-year-old Florence Meiler was recruited by her best friend to try track and field.

For 20 years, she and that friend, Barbara Jordan, trained and competed together, breaking world records at track-and-field events, as documented in an Oct. 30 Aging Untold talk show segment.

But 10 years ago, their time as athletic partners was cut short when Jordan was diagnosed with cancer and forced to stop sports altogether. Then, in 2020, Jordan died at age 85 after a five-year cancer journey.

Now 91, Meiler still thinks about her late best friend as she continues to break world records in her age group, 31 years after starting the sport.

"I miss her so much," the Shelburne, Vt., athlete told Aging Untold.

In a 2020 video profile aired as part of the Olympic Channel's Body+ series, Meiler elaborated on her longtime friendship.

"Training together was fantastic because we would challenge each other and we would criticize each other," the master athlete explained.

Pole vaults on a track field.Credit: Isaiah Vazquez/NCAA Photos via Getty

Meiler continued, "It's really a struggle for me to train by myself. It is a real struggle, but I have a determination, and I put Barb into my mind, and I've got to do this for Barb and I've got to do this for the state of Vermont."

That determination has since led her to break several world records in her 90–94 age group.

According to World Masters Athletics, Meiler holds world records for the 2,000-meter steeplechase (20:54.78), 80-meter hurdles (34.55), pole vault (1.30), high jump (0.92), triple jump (4.55) and pentathlon (2218).

"I really feel really warm inside because I've had so many athletes come up to me and say, 'What an inspiration you have been to me.' That's a wonderful feeling," she told Aging Untold.

As for her motto, Meiler told the Olympic Channel it's three simple words: "Never too late."

#2927. Dodgers’ Recent Rookie Standout Cuts Ties With New Team After Very Short Stint

The Los Angeles Dodgers are best known for acquiring high-priced free agent talent, but they've also made a habit of developing young players and finding ways to turn former prospects into valuable pieces elsewhere.

Still, not every promising player in the recent era has managed to sustain their early success in the major leagues. For one former Dodgers outfielder, a promising rookie campaign has now devolved into a series of team changes and roster moves.

His latest decision marks another opportunity to find a fresh start as he approaches the next stage of his career.

Braves Veteran Backstop Chadwick Tromp Cuts Ties With Dodgers After Very Short Stint

James Outman, who spent the first several seasons of his major-league career with Los Angeles, has now officially moved on from the Detroit Tigers after a brief run with the organization.

Outman elected free agency earlier this week, according to the official transaction log, ending his time in the Tigers organization after just five months.

Outman initially joined the Tigers as a waiver claim from the Minnesota Twins in early June. The 29-year-old subsequently received an opportunity with his new club, but his offensive production never developed into the kind of contribution the Tigers were hoping to find.

After being designated for assignment in August, Outman cleared waivers and was outrighted to Triple-A Toledo. He remained in the organization until the conclusion of the season, when he elected free agency.

"Outman hit just .183 in 100 plate appearances with Detroit," Charlie Wright wrote for MLB Trade Rumors. "He popped five home runs and stole a pair of bags. Outman saw time at all three outfield spots. He was a neutral defender by Defensive Runs Saved. Strikeouts continue to hold Outman back."

Giants 4-Year Veteran Tristan Beck Leaves MLB After Cut

The strikeout concerns have become a recurring issue for Outman, limiting his ability to establish himself as a consistent offensive contributor despite his power potential and defensive versatility.

Outman's departure from Detroit represents another chapter in a career that began with considerably more promise in Los Angeles.

The Dodgers selected Outman in the seventh round of the 2018 MLB Draft out of Sacramento State. He made his major-league debut in 2022 before breaking through as a rookie the following season.

In 2023, Outman appeared in 151 games for the Dodgers, batting .248 with 23 home runs, 70 RBI and 16 stolen bases. He earned National League Rookie of the Month honors twice, in April and August, establishing himself as an exciting young contributor in the Dodgers' lineup.

Those numbers have proven difficult to replicate. After struggling to maintain that production in subsequent seasons, Outman was traded to Minnesota in July 2025 in exchange for reliever Brock Stewart.

His time with the Twins eventually came to an end as well, leading to the move to Detroit earlier this year.

Now a free agent again, Outman will have another opportunity to find an organization willing to bet on the talent that once made him a promising Dodgers rookie.

#2926. Behind Steph Curry's late heroics, Warriors extinguish Magic comeback bid

Behind Steph Curry's late heroics, Warriors extinguish Magic comeback bid originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – Throughout the first half and well into the third quarter Saturday night, the Warriors, sloppy as they were, somehow maintained leads that threatened to mock the Orlando Magic and render the fourth quarter irrelevant.

But Orlando, ignoring its sub-mediocre status and Golden State's six-game win streak, got up and started swinging back in the third quarter, wiping out a double-digit deficit, taking leads and setting up a firefight to the finish.

That's when the Magic ran into Stephen Curry. He doesn't lose many firefights, and he made sure the Warriors didn't lose this one.

With Curry scoring 19 of his game-high 41 points in the fourth quarter and overtime, Yaxel Lendeborg saving his best for last, and getting timely defense down the stretch, the Warriors sweated out a 126-119 victory that pushed their win streak to seven and vaulted them into fifth place in the Western Conference.

"Tonight was good example that we have to we have to tighten up some things," coach Steve Kerr said. "But I'm thrilled with the grit and the togetherness and the way they fought back that was an amazing response to Orlando's great second half."

That amazing response in the fourth quarter and OT was built around Curry, who scored 14 points in the fourth – including the Warriors' final 12 points of regulation – and put them ahead for good with 1:38 left in OT.

"It's like a flow state," Curry said. "But also I was able to understand where the looks were going to come from because they're a really solid, athletic defensive team. They were blitzing a lot early, so I was trying to be patient. And then it became just try to find a matchup with driving lanes either way and then a lot of hit hand-backs for threes.

"I got to the foul line on crazy closeouts, so I was able to get in the lane. It's just more being decisive about how to get shots, make or miss or whatever. I think the clarity of exactly how to attack that particular defense tonight down the stretch was at an all-time high."

Though Lendeborg was marvelous down the stretch, with 16 of his 23 points in the fourth quarter and overtime, it was Curry who Orlando focused on stopping but could not.

"They have one of the best players in the world on their team," Magic wing Jamal Cain said. "And they're capitalizing at crunch time … we gave ourselves a chance to win, but they've got No. 30 on the other side."

Curry's 41 points came on 13-of-19 shooting from the field, including 5 of 10 from beyond the arc. He added six rebounds and six assists.

"It never gets old," Draymond Green said of Curry's heroics. "But he's so special. He always does something to where it can't get old. It's always something different."

Even as the Warriors rushed to a 14-point lead in the first eight minutes and raised it to 16 at the half, they did as much to lose as win. They gave the wounded Magic 26 points off 19 turnovers. They kept extending a hand to feed Orlando, which in the third quarter started biting it.

Golden State was outscored 37-20 in the third, with its 16-point halftime lead becoming a one-point (78-77) deficit entering the fourth.

"Our third quarter was just chaos," Kerr said.

"We actually got off to a better start today, and then I started turning the ball over," Green said. "And once I started turning the ball over, it was just a domino effect. I turned it over two or three times to start the second quarter, and then from that point on we were just kind of throwing the ball all over the place which then allowed them to get rhythm.

"Although it didn't happen right away – I think we went into the half up (16 points) – but what it did was it gave them hope."

The Magic sizzled in the third quarter, with 56-percent shooting from the field, including 50 percent from deep, providing 37 points. The Warriors recovered, holding Orlando to 36.1-percent shooting in the fourth quarter and overtime.

"They came out in the third quarter and kicked our butts," Kerr said. "But we let them off the hook, too, with some of the decision-making, carelessness of the passes. We just stopped executing. And if we want to be really good – and I think we can be – our number one goal is to become a team that can execute under pressure."

The best lessons for any team are those learned along the road to triumph. The gorgeous beginning led to a reckless middle before Curry, Lendeborg and the defense went to work, making sure the Warriors' best stuff didn't go to waste.

#2925. Berkshire shares surge as Wall Street swoons amid AI fears

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway shares roared past the S&P 500 index this week as investors moved money away from slumping tech stocks and moved into what appears to be the relative safety of a traditional conglomerate with relatively little exposure to artificial intelligence amid fears massive spending on data centers won't pay off.

One month ago, Berkshire's widely held B shares were underperforming the S&P by 13.6 percentage points for the year.

With help from three more record-high closes this week, Berkshire is now 5.8 percentage points ahead of the benchmark.

Berkshire more than doubled its share repurchases last quarter to $9.6 billion, while operating earnings declined slightly, the company said on Saturday.

Berkshire was up more than 5% this week while the S&P fell almost 2.7%.

The contrast is even sharper with the Nasdaq Composite, which fell 3.4% on the week.

Thursday's especially heavy tech selling pushed the index down more than 10% below its most recent high in what is commonly referred to as a "correction," but it did make up some of its lost ground Friday.

Two weeks ago, Berkshire's share prices completely erased all of the roughly 15% drop they suffered in the three months following Warren Buffett's surprise announcement at the 2025 shareholders meeting that he would step down as CEO at the end of that year.

Now they are up 6% from the record high closes they hit the Friday before Buffett's revelation on May 3, 2025.

Berkshire is not completely removed from the convulsions around AI.

Abel told us in early September he sees a "significant opportunity" ahead for Berkshire Hathaway Energy to provide electricity to data centers.

He also expected Berkshire would benefit from its big bet on Google parent Alphabet, saying he and Warren Buffett knew AI was "going to have a significant impact on America and businesses," and "we saw Google as a significant player."

Now that investment is getting negative reviews this week as the stock slumps along with other companies related to AI.

As of the end of June, Berkshire reported it owned almost 106 million shares of Alphabet, including 28.6 million shares it bought directly from the company in June: $5 billion of its Class A shares (GOOGL) for $351.81 each and another $5 billion of Class C shares (GOOG) for $348.20 each.

It bought another $7 billion of Alphabet shares on the open market during the second quarter, adding to the position it started building in last year's third quarter, an investment initiated by Warren Buffett.

Bloomberg suggests that "with cracks in the AI trade starting to show, Berkshire's sudden push [into AI] may appear ill-timed."

On June 30, the position had a market value of $37.8 billion and it was the third largest equity holding in Berkshire's portfolio.

Since then both the Class A and Class C shares have dropped by around 20% and the stake is now valued at $30.2 billion, falling behind Coca-Cola's $38.3 billion to the portfolio's #4 position.

(In just over a week, we'll find out whether Berkshire made any changes to its Alphabet position during the third quarter ending September 30.)

Bloomberg quotes CFRA Research analyst Cathy Seifert as saying, "I do think that part of this may be a conscious decision to increase their exposure. One has to ask the question though, is it a little late?"

But the article notes that "for dedicated value investors and longtime Berkshire observers, such jitters may not really matter," with Keefe Bruyette & Woods analyst Meyer Shields saying Abel probably sees bets on AI as a long-term wager on the technology's potential, in keeping with the "conservatism that you would expect at Berkshire Hathaway."

At Barron's, however, Andrew Bary sees what he estimates is a $3 billion loss on Alphabet as part of a "disappointing" long-term trend in Berkshire's stock choices

"The company hasn't created much value from stock picking during the current decade" even though the "S&P 500 has returned 150% since the end of 2019 and tech stocks have quadrupled."

While Berkshire's investments in five Japanese "trading houses," Chevron, and Chubb have done well, "there have been plenty of losers or simply mediocre investments that have been closed out."

He cites Verizon, HP, Paramount (now Skydance), General Motors, Constellation Brands, and Pool.

While he estimates Berkshire's Occidental Petroleum investment is up about 20% from what it paid, OXY is "far behind most energy stocks" amid this year's big increase in crude prices.

Bary also lists stocks that have continued to rise in price after Berkshire cut its stakes, with Apple being the worst offender.

That stake has been reduced by 75% since 2023 at what Bary calculates is an average selling price around $200 per share, well below AAPL's current $342.

Despite those sales, Apple is Berkshire's largest equity holding with a market value of $78 billion. The stock is up 1200% since Berkshire started buying it at the beginning of 2016, at a time when many thought the company had already seen its best days.

Bary thinks Abel will probably keep Berkshire's core equity investments but may concentrate more on his strengths going forward: improving Berkshire's operating companies and making new acquisitions.

His conclusion: "Barring a market rout, equity investing may not be central to Berkshire anymore.

"And if any new Berkshire holdings are revealed soon, it may not be a good idea to buy them. The record hasn't been great."

Berkshire Hathaway's disclosure that it had made a relatively small $27 million addition to its $4.1 billion Sirius XM position helped fuel an ongoing rally that lifted the satellite radio company's shares to a 52-week high this week.

The stock ended 4.9% higher that day at $28.67.

On Friday, Monday, and Tuesday, Berkshire bought more than 924 thousand shares at an average price of $29.10 as the stock continued to gain ground, ending Tuesday at $30.80.

Berkshire disclosed the buys late Tuesday evening, apparently helping to fuel an additional 8.4% gain as the stock closed at $33.40 on Wednesday.

It hit a 52-week intraday high of $33.76 on Thursday but finished lower Friday at $32.40.

Overall, Sirius XM is up 18.6% since the earnings release and has gained 62.0% so far this year.

It's the first addition to Berkshire's stake since late-July and early-August last year.

In 2025, it bought a total of 7.3 million shares at an average price of $21.86.

The position is likely one of portfolio manager Ted Weschler's stocks.

Pacific Power, a unit of Berkshire Hathaway's PacifiCorp, is asking California regulators for a 57% rate increase that would raise the typical residential electric bill by $120 a month.

The Wall Street Journal calls it a "stunning request even in an era of rising electricity costs."

The utility says the hike would be partially offset by a 14% decrease taking effect on January 1.

A lot of the $103 million that would be generated by the increase is already being spent on efforts to make power equipment in the sparsely populated and heavily wooded area more resistant to wildfires.

The utility is facing around $637 million in liability claims for fires in 2020 and 2022.

This week, Reuters reports, Oregon's Supreme Court heard arguments on whether to revive a jury verdict that could expose PacifiCorp "to tens of billions of dollars of potential damages over its role in four Oregon wildfires in 2020."

Warren Buffett and Charlie Munger share their opinions about how artificial intelligence could affect the economy, the government, and how much time people spend on leisure activities.

ANDREW ROSS SORKIN: Thomas Kimay (PH) is here. He's a 27-year-old shareholder from Kentfield, California.

And I should preface this question by saying that he was here 17 years ago at 10 years old, asked you a question from the audience asking you if the internet might hurt some of Berkshire's investments.

At the time, you said you wanted to see how things would play out. He's now updated the question. (Buffett laughs)

"What do you think about the implications of artificial intelligence on Berkshire's businesses, beyond autonomous driving and GEICO, which you've talked about already? ...

WARREN BUFFETT: I certainly have no special insights on artificial intelligence, but I will bet a lot of things happen in that field in the next couple of decades, and probably a shorter timeframe.

They should lead, I would certainly think — but again, I don't bring much to this party. But I would certainly think they would result in significantly less employment in certain areas.

And it may not be good for a given business, but let's take it to the extreme.

Let's assume one person could push a button and, essentially, through various machines and robotics, all kinds of things, turn out all of the output we have in this country.

So, everybody's — there's just as much output as we have. It's all being done by, you know, instead of 150-some million people being employed, one person.

You know, is the world better off or not?

Well, certainly we'd work a lot less hours a week — of work per week and so on.

I mean, it would be a good thing, but it would require enormous transformation in how people relate to each other, what they expect of government, you know, all kinds of things. And, of course, as a practical matter, more than one person would keep working.

But pushing the idea that way is one of the — you'd certainly think that's one of the consequences of making great progress in artificial intelligence.

And that's enormously prosocial, eventually. It's enormously disruptive in other ways. And it can have huge problems, in terms of a democracy and how it reacts to that...

CHARLIE MUNGER: Well, you're painting a very funny world where everybody's engaged in trade. And the trade is, I give you golf lessons and you dye my hair. And that would be a world kind of like the royal family of Kuwait or something.

And I don't think it would be good for America to have everything produced by one person and the rest of us just engaged in leisure.

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,222,245,324,463

Berkshire Cash as of June 30: $365.5 billion (Down 8.0% from March 31)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $359.2 billion (Down 3.8% from March 31)

Berkshire repurchased $4.5 billion of its shares in Q2 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.
