#2924. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2922. Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg surprises mom with new house in amazing moment

Golden State Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg has always been vocal about the love for his mother, Yissel Raposo. Together, they shed tears of joy when his name was called at the No. 11 pick in the 2026 NBA Draft to the Warriors in June.

Lendeborg has been vocal about the sacrifices that his mother made for him and one of his goals was to pay her back. He debuted a new YouTube channel showcasing "behind-the-scenes, life outside of basketball and everything in between" where a video released on Friday, Nov. 6 had a surprise reveal of a recently purchased house for his mother.

"On behalf of everybody that came to show up, welcome home," Lendeborg said to Raposo, moving her from New Jersey to California.

Raposo was speechless as happy tears trickled down her face and she took the keys out of a box Lendeborg handed her and she opened the door as they took a tour of her new home.

Lendeborg said it took him 2 to 3 months to finalize the house. To keep his mom from finding out, he and his sister would often disguise phone conversations around visiting Hawaii; instead, they were talking about how to get their mom to her new house without suspicion.

"There is no gesture big enough to properly say thank you for all the love, sacrifice, and support my mom has poured into me but this moment was pretty special and one I'll never forget," Lendeborg said in his video description. "She deserves it all. For me, it's bigger than basketball. It's about making sure the people who believed in me get to enjoy the blessings too."

Warriors head coach Steve Kerr called the gesture "incredible."

"I saw it this morning," Kerr said on Nov. 7 before the Warriors' 126-119 win against the Orlando Magic. "Just such a heartwarming moment. It's life-changing for these guys to make the NBA. Life-changing for them and their families. You always love to see someone like Yaxel taking care of his mom. Taking care of his family."

Lendeborg has always discussed the bond that he and his mother share and how she's directly responsible for his achievements in basketball and finding early success in the NBA.

"I got here because of her," Lendeborg said in June after getting drafted. "She pushed a dream, forced me to go out there and step into the world and become a man. I had no choice but to go out there and do the best I could. So I vowed every day to be a better man, be a better person and just do the best I can for her."

Sharing the moments of success with his mom means everything to Lendeborg because of the sacrifices that she made for him and his sisters, having to work multiple jobs to ensure they have what they need. Raposo also battled Stage 4 appendix cancer, another thing that fuels Lendeborg.

"She put her whole life on hold for my life and I'm super proud of her for fighting so hard and I'm glad we get to share this moment together," he said in a June interview after being drafted.

Lendeborg has been a bright spot for the Warriors on the court. The Warriors extended their winning streak to seven games after defeating the Magic on Saturday, Nov. 7 at Chase Center in San Francisco, California. Lendeborg scored 23 points on 9-of-15 field goal shooting, adding 8 rebounds and three blocks.

The rookie averages 21.2 points, 7.2 rebounds, and 4.0 assists per game through his first 10 games of the 2026–27 NBA season.

#2921. Game Preview #11: Wolves vs Jazz Game Thread

The Minnesota Timberwolves return home to take on a Utah Jazz team in search of a rhythm. Minnesota is riding a three-game win streak and has found their cadence on defense while getting up the court quickly on offense. This marks the start of eight straight games in Minnesota after a grueling first two and a half weeks of the season. Hopefully, the fans at Target Center can match the Wolves' tempo.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (7-3) vs. Utah Jazz (1-8)

The promise of this Jazz team has yet to come to fruition. Utah has won only one game and is bad. They are 29th in offensive rating and 23rd in defensive rating. They are a very young team. The starting veterans they do have are injured.

They are sloppy with the basketball and give it away over 17 times a game on average. Meanwhile, the Wolves boast a top-three defense and are top-10 in generating turnovers.

Ayo Dosunmu has been living by his Jet Life mantra. He is also forcing the most steals of his career. When he pokes the ball away or sees any semblance of open floor, he is breaking the sound barrier on the way to the hoop.

NEW ORLEANS, LA – NOVEMBER 6: Ayo Dosunmu #13 of the Minnesota Timberwolves drives to the basket during the game against the New Orleans Pelicans during the 2026 Emirates NBA Cup on November 6, 2026 at the Smoothie King Center in New Orleans, Louisiana. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Layne Murdoch Jr./NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The Wolves lost Joan Beringer for the latter half of the game against the New Orleans Pelicans and reportedly suffered a nasal fracture. Trey Lyles did his admirable best to hold down the center spot as Rudy Gobert sat, but he doesn't offer the same chaotic disruption on the boards.

There is potential for Joan to return against the Jazz wearing a protective mask. Should Beringer miss this game, even with the Jazz front court depleted, the Wolves will struggle to get necessary extra possessions.

Anthony Edwards, Donte DiVincenzo, and TJ Shannon Jr. are all still listed as out.

Continuing to lead the attack with all those absences is LaMelo Ball, who has found his shot and has scored 20 or more points in four of the last five games. He has hit shots from deep and at the rim. In the Wolves' most recent bout, he turned around and took Zion Williamson to the cup again and again. He has the handle and the threat to score from anywhere, putting any of the Jazz defenders in compromising positions. His production will be key once again.

The Jazz starting guards Keyonte George and second-overall-pick Darryn Peterson have yet to discover harmony and sound more like H. John Benjamin's Jazz Daredevil album than anything you might find on Bourbon St, the Jazz' original home (and where they should still be if we're being honest).

Nov 4, 2026; New Orleans, Louisiana, USA; Utah Jazz guard Keyonte George (3) acknowledges New Orleans Pelicans center Derik Queen (22) after an overtime game at Smoothie King Center. Mandatory Credit: Matthew Hinton-Imagn Images | IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Each guard takes turns looking for their own shot when sharing the court, often in the midrange. Seeing this, head coach Will Hardy has turned to splitting up the duo with a mix of starters and bench players.

The Jazz do rebound the ball well, at least on defense. Currently, they are top five in grabbing defensive boards but struggle mightily to recover their own misses. Going against a Wolves team hungry to finish plays or get another chance at a shot, they may find themselves in an early hole.

Minnesota has impressed with their second efforts for offensive rebounds and has managed to maintain the same aggression late into the game when their opponents look tired while the Wolves look ravenous.

In the years since trading Donovan Mitchell to the Cleveland Cavaliers and Rudy Gobert to the Wolves, the Jazz have worked, almost conspiratorially, to lose games and improve their lottery odds. Tanking can lead to selecting a franchise-altering star. It can also lead to a pernicious culture of bad habits, the sort of habits that produce gaudy stats and few wins.

Utah's guards and wings possess the ability to generate their own shot but offer little resistance on the other side of the ball. When a team does puncture their limited-effort defense, they offer even less resistance at the rim.

Both Jazz center options, former Defensive Player of the Year Jaren Jackson Jr. and Jusuf Nurkić, from last year's team are injured. In their stead are journeymen bigs Jaxson Hayes and Mo Bamba.

Speaking of journeymen, Josh Okogie returns to Minnesota as Utah's best point-of-attack defender. Alongside him, second-year forward Ace Bailey has held his own on defense as he waits for one of his point guards to include him on offense. If either Okogie or Ace gets pulled into help, Jonathan Kuminga and Jaden McDaniels will feast on open looks.

Nov 2, 2026; Minneapolis, Minnesota, USA; Milwaukee Bucks forward Jaime Jaquez Jr. (24) works between Minnesota Timberwolves forward Jonathan Kuminga (24) and forward Jaden McDaniels (3) in the fourth quarter at Target Center. Mandatory Credit: Bruce Kluckhohn-Imagn Images | IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

The Timberwolves get out on the fast break and leave the Jazz chasing Minnesota's tempo. The Target Center becomes more raucous as the home team continues to build their lead. Joan Beringer returns with a Phantom of the Opera mask and makes Mo Bamba look like Less Bamba. Isaiah Evans hits two threes in garbage time to balloon the lead back to 20+.

#2920. America doesn't like socialism. So why are socialists winning?

Democratic socialists just had their biggest election night yet.

There's just one complication: Most American voters don't actually like socialism.

Six candidates backed by the Democratic Socialists of America won House seats Tuesday, giving the movement its largest foothold yet in Congress and greater clout in a Democratic Party already divided over how far left to go.

The victories, coming on the heels of New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani's rise to power, mark another milestone for a movement that has spent years challenging the party establishment. Now, with Democrats sweeping control of Congress, the question is whether those electoral gains will translate into actual political power.

The results of the new CNBC Voter Poll show the potential hazard of handing too much power to the party's left flank. The survey of roughly 80,000 midterm voters found that 53% view socialism unfavorably, compared with 33% who viewed it favorably. Capitalism fared considerably better, with 56% expressing a positive view. Just 18% of voters identified themselves as democratic socialists.

That disconnect underscores both the opportunity and the challenge facing Democrats and their growing left flank. How the party reconciles the divide between reforming capitalism and fundamentally overhauling it could shape its legislative agenda in Congress, its economic message to voters and, ultimately, its direction heading into the 2028 presidential election.

The DSA's presence in Congress will be quadrupled when Tuesday's victors, all of whom won in safely Democratic districts, are sworn in.

But the projected Democratic landslide of more than 250 House seats could dilute their influence. Party leaders could afford to lose all eight DSA votes on most measures, leaving the group with far less bargaining power than it would wield in a narrowly controlled chamber.

Still, the movement is building financial muscle. While the DSA relies on membership dues, wealthy donors are now backing its candidates through outside groups. The 12 largest donors to American Priorities, a super PAC supporting some DSA-backed candidates, gave nearly $5.9 million this cycle, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings through Oct. 31.

"We've had success with more donors but also we have an even stronger ground game to spread our message," said Megan Romer, co-chair of the DSA. "Mailers and ads alone just don't do it anymore. You have to knock on more doors and make more phone calls and talk to more people at work and in your union."

The more revealing tests on Tuesday came outside of liberal enclaves.

In Florida, DSA member Angie Nixon came within three percentage points of an upset in a state where Democrats haven't won a Senate race since 2012 and President Donald Trump won by 13 percentage points in 2024. But she lost by a wider margin than Democratic gubernatorial candidate David Jolly, who took a more moderate approach.

Michigan told a similar story. Progressive Abdul El-Sayed – backed by self-described democratic socialist Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., – won his Senate seat by seven percentage points. Yet he lagged nearly four percentage points behind Democratic Gov.-elect Jocelyn Benson's winning margin.

El-Sayed shares many DSA priorities but neither belongs to the group nor received its endorsement. He says he favors reforming capitalism over replacing it.

State ballot initiatives offered an equally mixed ideological verdict: Nearly two-thirds of voters in ruby-red Montana backed limits on corporate political spending, while deep-blue California rejected a billionaire wealth tax by roughly 10 percentage points, even as voters approved higher income taxes on the wealthy.

The results sharpen the dilemma over Democrats' identity heading into 2028, as Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, D-N.Y., the DSA's most prominent standard-bearer, seemingly weighs a White House bid: Can left-wing populism expand the party's coalition without alienating the swing-state moderates it needs to win a presidential election – especially in a less favorable political environment?

"I think a lot of people did vote for Democrats who would not ordinarily and who probably did it ... with their teeth gritted," Romer told CNBC.

But it's clear that even voters who reject socialism aren't satisfied with capitalism as it stands.

Three out of every four participants in the CNBC Voter Poll said the condition of the U.S. economy was poor or not so good, compared with just 2% who described it as excellent. And 70% said the economic system favors the wealthy rather than treating most Americans fairly.

That frustration helped fuel a populist message far beyond the socialist left, with victorious Democrats from Adam Hamilton in Kansas to James Talarico in Texas tapping into anger over rising costs, economic inequality and the political influence of wealthy elites. Eighteen Sanders-endorsed candidates also won, including in traditionally right-leaning states like Ohio and Montana.

"The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom," Talarico, who flipped a Senate seat for Democrats in the Lone Star state, said in his victory speech. "The people at the top work so hard to keep us angry and divided because our unity is a threat to their wealth and their power."

Even politicians who weren't on the ballot took note. Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a video posted to X Friday that "Democrats won by being populist economic fighters."

Economists have pointed to measures showing increasing wealth concentration and a shrinking share of the U.S. GDP heading to workers in the form of pay as signs that the divide between the country's rich and poor is widening. Coming out of the pandemic, the "K"-shaped economy has become a common descriptor for the unequal recovery seen by income classes.

Americans displayed their economic dissatisfaction well before they headed to the polls this week. The University of Michigan's closely followed consumer sentiment index hit all-time lows this year.

"We have a rising tide that is not equally lifting all boats," said Joe Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM, a tax firm. "That's not what we used to tell ourselves when we were young."

Rather than signaling a distaste for capitalism as a whole, Brusuelas said this week's results show voters want a system that also includes populist policies like Medicare for all. Or, as he calls it, "capitalism with a human face."

"These things are not mutually exclusive," said Brusuelas, who regularly briefs members of Congress on policy issues. "We can walk and chew gum at the same time."

Tuesday's Democratic sweep left some on Wall Street racing to prepare their clients' portfolios for potential policy shifts. Bank of America analysts detailed what heightened scrutiny of social media platforms or artificial intelligence data centers could mean for the shares of companies in those industries. CIBC built a basket of stocks that should benefit from a Democratically controlled Congress.

Still, the reaction of Wall Street veterans and New York City business moguls to this week's election was relatively subdued compared with Mamdani's victory in the mayoral race last year.

After Mamdani clinched his party's nomination, Pershing Square CEO Bill Ackman said he was "a bit depressed." Ahead of Mamdani's victory, Kathryn Wylde, who leads the Partnership for New York City, a nonprofit group of business executives, told CNBC that "terror is the feeling" as he rose in the polls.

Wall Street magnates wondered how a city known as the epicenter of financial markets and global capitalism could survive under a self-described socialist. Some questioned if billionaires would flee the city or whether corporations would move their headquarters — fears that largely haven't materialized since Mamdani took office.

With roughly 10 months of Mamdani's term in the rearview mirror, Anthony Chan, the former chief economist at JPMorgan, said finance moguls have realized that politics don't necessarily result in material policy changes. Chan said it's a message they've carried with them exiting this week, with many understanding that any incoming lawmakers in Washington aligned with democratic socialist policies would be a small minority.

"Many of the people that I meet that were very adamantly against and opposed to his candidacy now are sending their kids to his free daycare centers," Chan said, referencing Mamdani's partnership with the state to expand no-cost programs for 2-year-olds in New York City.

"I thought you felt that the world was going to end if this mayor came in," said Chan, who's also an alumnus of Barclays and the New York Federal Reserve. "Now, you're sending your kids to his free daycare centers."

But the DSA has even bigger ambitions for 2028. With growing political clout, infrastructure and financial backing, the movement is looking to expand its influence nationwide, while one of its most prominent allies, Ocasio-Cortez, weighs a White House run.

"That infrastructure is there, and that will make a big difference for a potential left presidential candidate," Romer said. "It's really kind of do or die time. We really have to deliver."

#2918. Ravens film study: Zion Young stepping up after Tavius Robinson injury

The Buffalo Bills handled the Baltimore Ravens 36-10 in Buffalo last Sunday, in a game that quickly went downhill after starting quarterback Lamar Jackson and backup Tyler Huntley both suffered potentially season-ending injuries. Despite the lopsided score, Baltimore's defense showed some signs of life, with two players standing out on tape. Roquan Smith arguably put together his best performance as a Raven, while rookie Zion Young continued to show why he could be an important piece of Baltimore's defense moving forward.

35 snaps | 21 run defense | 14 pass rush | 1 tackle | 1 assist | 1 pressure

Zion Young's box score may not immediately stand out, but turning on the tape reveals the type of impact that makes his long-term outlook so intriguing. Young finished with 1 tackle, 1 assist and 1 pressure while recording a 100% pass-rush win rate on true pass sets. While the statistical production was limited, his ability to affect running lanes, displace blockers and maintain gap integrity was evident throughout the afternoon. Young continues to show that his value extends well beyond traditional box-score production.

One of Young's most impressive traits throughout the season has been his ability to work down the line of scrimmage against the run, and Sunday was no different. On one rep, Young worked laterally down the line and made a key tackle to prevent Josh Allen from sneaking into the end zone. On another, he flashed his physicality and hand usage, displacing a blocker and forcing the running back to redirect into a different rushing lane. Perhaps his most impressive rep came when he engaged two blockers simultaneously and drove both into the backfield, disrupting the timing of the run and allowing the rest of Baltimore's defense to rally to the football.

With Tavius Robinson sidelined, Young is expected to assume a larger role as Baltimore's starting outside linebacker on early downs. His ability to set a firm edge, maintain gap discipline and use his length and power at the point of attack makes him a natural fit for that responsibility. Young has consistently demonstrated the technique, physicality and effort necessary to succeed as an early-down defender. The next step in his development will be expanding his opportunities as a pass rusher, where his power profile and developing arsenal of moves offer intriguing upside. As his workload continues to increase, Young has an opportunity to establish himself as a foundational piece of Baltimore's defensive front.

52 snaps | 25 run defense | 3 pass rush | 24 coverage | 8 tackles | 4 assists | 1 pressure | 1 forced fumble

Roquan Smith arguably had his best performance as a Raven against Buffalo, flying around the field in coverage, flowing downhill against the run and making key tackles both in the backfield and in open space. Smith finished with 8 tackles, 4 assists, 1 pressure and a forced fumble while allowing just 1 reception for 16 yards in coverage. One of the most impressive aspects of his performance was his ability to consistently put himself in position to make plays, showing the instincts, physicality and range that have made him one of the league's premier linebackers.

On one rep, Smith flashed his ability as a power rusher, knocking back a Buffalo offensive guard and driving him into Josh Allen's lap. Against the run, Smith consistently displayed excellent spatial awareness, navigating around blockers to keep himself clean and meet running backs in the hole. On a toss play to James Cook, Smith showed excellent tackling technique and body control to bring him down in open space. His biggest play of the afternoon came on a shot over the middle, where he rallied to the football and punched it loose to force a fumble that Baltimore recovered.

After receiving a pregame speech from Ravens legend Ray Lewis, Smith delivered arguably his most complete performance of the season. His play over the past several weeks has been inconsistent, particularly compared to the standard he has established throughout his career. However, Sunday's performance was an encouraging reminder of the impact he can have when playing with confidence and decisiveness. If Smith can build on this performance, it could go a long way toward helping Baltimore's defense return to form.

#2915. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

President Donald Trump said Saturday that he was ordering the Justice Department to investigate Democratic leaders over what he described as threats against Republican officials, donors and supporters, without identifying the alleged communications or providing evidence of criminal conduct.

"The Democrats are going around threatening a lot of people, including the Attorney General of the United States, and others, with Impeachment and many other forms of punishment, even though they admit they have no reason to do so," Trump said in a Truth Social post.

The alleged threats, the president said, are "extortion at the highest level," he wrote. "I am ordering the Department of Justice to look into this criminal act, immediately!"

Trump was apparently referencing a speech Slotkin made at a University of Chicago event on Oct. 13. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Slotkin alleges that she, her family and her staff faced physical and legal intimidation after the posting of a November 2025 video in which Slotkin and other Democratic leaders called on the U.S. military to "refuse illegal orders" following the airstrikes targeting boats in the Caribbean.

The FBI contacted lawmakers, including Slotkin, days later. In February, Slotkin declined to sit for an interview the Justice Department requested about the video.

"The intimidation is the point, and I'm not gonna go along with that," the senator said in February. A federal grand jury later declined to indict the video participants.

Now, Trump alleges Democrats are threatening legal intimidation against Republican leaders and their supporters.

"Republicans should get smart, fast! Threatening letters have been sent to many people, all innocent, from Democrat Political Leaders," the president wrote.

The White House and Slotkin didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

#2910. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2908. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after U.S. Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether the administration still has support to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline-driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Late Saturday, U.S. Central Command said it disabled a St. Kitts-flagged ship after the tanker ignored multiple warnings and attempted to leave the region after visiting an Iranian port, according to media reports. Iran state media also reported on explosions on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, as well as several blasts occurring in the port of Jask. CNBC couldn't independently confirm the reports.

Several oil tankers have also come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, traded above $97 on Friday, while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump no longer faces future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution anyway.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11, and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

#2906. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2905. Meta chief strategy officer Dave Wehner leaves to join AI infrastructure startup Fireworks as CFO

Dave Wehner, Meta's chief strategy officer, is leaving the company for four-year-old AI startup Fireworks.

At Fireworks — an AI inference and model-training platform focused on open models that was valued at $17.5 billion during a Series D fundraising round in July — Wehner will assume the CFO role, he announced on X on Friday.

Wehner will be tasked with scaling the company's finances and operations, Fireworks CEO Lin Qiao said in a statement to CNBC.

"Fireworks has the right mission for the moment," Wehner wrote on X, adding: "I've become increasingly bullish on what they are building. There's a remarkable depth of technical talent here, coupled with a very practical focus on what customers need."

The AI startup, founded in 2022 by former Meta and Google employees, is a platform that lets developers customize and run open models for their businesses. Its latest investment round was led by Atreides Management, Index Ventures and TCV, but Fireworks is also backed by Nvidia and Sequoia Capital. Business Insider reported that it is in early talks to raise funds again at a possible $30 billion to $40 billion valuation.

Wehner joined Meta, then called Facebook, in 2012 and was the company's chief financial officer from 2014 to 2022.

"Dave helped guide Meta through tremendous growth and complexity over 14 years, and brought disciplined thinking to some of our hardest company-level decisions," Meta COO Javier Olivan said in a statement to CNBC.

Fireworks is notably smaller than Meta — the tech giant has about a $1.56 trillion market capitalization as of Friday's close — and its competitors, which include OpenAI and Anthropic.

"Dave told me he wanted to get back to a smaller company where he could build toward a big impact again," Fireworks' Lin wrote in a LinkedIn post on Friday. "He sees AI as the defining technology shift of his lifetime and wants to help shape what comes next."

The company's size has not made it immune to the risks and complications often associated with AI technology. In October, the company found "a security incident involving unauthorized use of internal Fireworks credentials," in which "an unauthorized third party accessed environment variables used in evaluation jobs in a limited number of customer accounts," it said in a press release.

"Based on our investigation at this time, we have not detected evidence of unauthorized access to customer models, datasets, training data, personal information, or inference traffic within the Fireworks security perimeter or control," the release said.

#2903. 31-year-old dumpster diver brought in $140,000 in 12 months: I 'did much more than I thought was possible'

When Carter Stevens tells people he makes six figures a year digging through dumpsters, they often assume he's "blowing smoke," he says.

Not everyone sees dollar signs in a brimming dumpster. But Stevens earned roughly $140,000 in total sales between September 2025 and September 2026 by salvaging trashed items, cleaning them up and selling them on platforms like eBay and Facebook Marketplace, according to documents reviewed by CNBC Make It.

Stevens primarily operates near office parks and luxury condominiums around Nashville, Tennessee. Since March 2024, when he started dumpster diving, he's found and sold hand-crafted Persian rugs, high-end office chairs and even samurai swords, he says. Trash-picking helps him keep his shipping costs low, he adds: Some of his supplies, like cardboard boxes and packing tape, also come from dumpsters, enough that he doesn't have to pay for them approximately "90% of the time."

"The amount of waste in this country that you'll find is unbelievable. We throw so much stuff out," says Stevens, 31.

Sometimes, Stevens asks a building for permission to go through its trash, marketing himself as a junk removal service. In that way, his job is similar — though not identical, because he generally only takes what he can sell, he says — to other entrepreneurs across the U.S. who follow advice in Reddit threads or YouTube tutorials to launch junk removal side hustles or full-time businesses. There are around 10,000 junk removal businesses across the U.S., according to the Better Business Bureau. They range in scale from independent side hustles to national companies like 1-800-GOT-JUNK, which touts more than 150 franchise locations.

Other times, Stevens finds himself neck deep in rubbish, ducking concerned security guards, peeling away actual trash and rotting food in the hope of uncovering discarded treasure, he says. He tries to avoid sneaking around and keeps an eye out for "no trespassing" signs. He'd rather leave items behind if a property owner or security guard tells him to vacate the premises, he says.

Even Stevens expresses surprise at how much money he's made, saying he doesn't necessarily think his annual income is sustainable. When he "stumbled into" dumpster diving, he was approximately $12,000 in debt after years of poor spending habits fueled partially by a drug addiction, a stark change from his affluent childhood, he says.

"I dug myself out of a hole. I built up my savings. I really did much more than I thought was possible," says Stevens.

Stevens, a Nashville native, is the son of a five-time Grammy Award-winning producer of country and gospel music who has worked with artists like Blake Shelton and Carrie Underwood. His life took a turn in college when he developed an addiction to prescription Adderall, leading him to drop out partway through his senior year in 2018, he says.

His lack of a college degree made landing full-time work a challenge. He says his parents can afford to support him financially, and they've done so in specific emergency occasions. But mostly, his father wants his kids to learn to fend for themselves, he says: "He will let me sit on the coals for the sake of learning how to swim. That's taught me a lot [and] given me a lot of valuable skills and a tough mentality."

Unemployed, Stevens started combing through thrift shops and Goodwill clothing racks for vintage apparel to sell on eBay. He was inspired by YouTube influencers like Rick Senko, he says, whose Fort Lauderdale, Florida-based clothing resale business Technsports brought in $6.5 million in annual revenue as recently as 2024.

But Stevens' returns were inconsistent, and he often spent his profits on drugs or luxury shopping sprees, he says. After his parents funded a rehab stint in 2021 — helping him "quit doing the hard drugs," he says — he spent his money chasing other dopamine hits. Those indulgences included a $1,200 pair of Christian Dior Birkenstock sandals, an 85-inch flat-screen television and a $30,000 down payment on a Mercedes-AMG sedan that he later lost because he couldn't afford the total cost, he says.

By June 2023, he could no longer afford to pay rent, so he lived in a rented storage unit until the business's security found and evicted him, and then stayed in his Nissan Sentra for three weeks, he says. Homelessness shocked him into learning to manage his "reckless" spending habits, he says: "Being punished by your own spending addiction was a wringer that I promised myself I can't be put through again."

In October 2023, a cousin lent him money to start renting an apartment, and Stevens worked to stick to a sensible enough budget for his thrifting income to cover future rent and basic expenses, he says. Five months later, he found a discarded pair of black Nike Air Force Ones in good condition next to a gas station pump. He took them home, sanitized them and sold them online for $35, he says.

"That was the first time I had ever found something that was trash and sold it and made a little bit of money," says Stevens.

Once he started dumpster diving, Stevens was hooked "because I never stopped finding good stuff in these dumpsters," he says.

His biggest score to date: dozens of discarded Steelcase office chairs, worth $1,000-plus each when new, outside an overflowing office park dumpster, he says. He got his car — by this point, a used pickup truck and a self-built trailer — and hauled away 190 chairs to list online at hundreds of dollars apiece. He sold the majority of them in bulk transactions on Facebook Marketplace for $17,000, and brought in an additional $10,600 selling the remaining chairs individually, documents show.

If Stevens manages to find roughly one valuable haul per month, he can fill his $400-a-month storage unit and start listing the items. For the most part, he scopes out corporate environments, preferring "dry dumpsters" over ones laden with food waste, he says. Office trash is particularly profitable when companies downsize, says Stevens: Once, he found 120 standing desks discarded outside a building.

Near high-end apartment buildings, Stevens has found furniture from brands like West Elm, iPads, electronics and power tools in working condition, he says. "Almost every piece of furniture in my apartment came from a dumpster," says Stevens.

Stevens works alone and minimizes gas costs by sticking to locations within a few miles from his apartment. The majority of his income has come from big hauls across his five highest-earning months, reflecting the venture's hit-or-miss nature. "I got really lucky earlier this year and I made a ton of money. I'm not acting like this is something I could do every year," he says.

So he saves as much of his earnings as possible, "constantly" talking himself out of unnecessary purchases, he says. And he's studying the business of real estate wholesaling — which involves securing contracts for distressed properties and flipping them to investors for a fee — as a potentially more secure way to earn a living, he adds.

Still, he acknowledges the allure of knowing his next big payday could always be one dumpster away.

"I don't see myself ever turning down a good looking dumpster," says Stevens. "I have found so much cool stuff that I feel like I'll always be curious about what's in that dumpster."

#2902. Mark Cuban says he doesn’t ‘give a f---' about growing his net worth—a healthy mindset for non-billionaires too, a financial therapist says

Mark Cuban has zero F's to give — at least, when it comes to his net worth. Financial therapists say that's a healthy mindset to have, even if you're not a multibillionaire like Cuban.

"I don't give a f--- about my net worth. I have enough. My family has enough," Cuban posted Monday on social media platform X, responding to another user's post about the growth of the serial entrepreneur's wealth going back to 1990.

"I don't care about moving up on the Forbes 400," Cuban added.

Cuban currently has an estimated net worth of $6.8 billion, according to Forbes, which ranks the former star of ABC's "Shark Tank" as the 597th wealthiest person in the world. Cuban sold his first business, a software company called MicroSolutions, for $6 million in 1990. He became a billionaire when he and his co-founders sold Broadcast.com to Yahoo for $5.7 billion in 1999.

He developed his attitude after buying a majority stake in the NBA's Dallas Mavericks in January 2000, he tells CNBC Make It: "I was more interested in winning than making money." The mindset might seem obvious to some: After all, why would a billionaire have any stress about his bank account? But even people with lots of money experience financial stress and anxiety, studies have shown.

What seems to set Cuban apart from some other wealthy people is the confidence he exhibits in how he puts his wealth to use, says Christine Hargrove, an Athens, Georgia-based financial therapist. In his post, Cuban wrote that he doesn't obsess over growing his personal net worth because, in part, he'd rather see the impact his money can have on the world.

"My next dollar can impact other people's lives a lot more than mine," wrote Cuban, citing his decision to launch the cost-cutting pharmacy startup Cost Plus Drugs and his many small-business investments across 15 seasons on "Shark Tank" as an investor judge. "That is what drives my investments. I look for ways to have an impact."

Confidence in how you save or spend your money is often a signal of a healthy financial mindset, notes Hargrove, who is president-elect of the Financial Therapy Association and Assistant Director of the Love and Money Center at the University of Georgia.

"You actually have to be the person to decide what is enough for you," she says, adding: "Having a sense of purpose with your money and with your life can be an incredible source of protection against chronic financial anxiety."

Many Americans, especially as costs of living remain high nationwide, have no choice but to worry about their finances.

When those with plenty of money in their bank accounts or stock portfolios obsess over their level of wealth, they're also probably not doing so by choice, notes Hargrove. Some people's financial insecurities come from growing up in low-income households. Others may think of themselves as relatively struggling because they earn less than the other wealthy people around them.

"Having a healthy relationship with money isn't just about how much you have. It's about understanding what money means to you and what you want it to do for you," says Cara Macksoud, an Orlando-based accredited financial counselor and financial behavior specialist.

No matter what your financial situation is, anxiety about money can actually hurt your finances in addition to the toll it takes on your mental health. Financial stress and related mental health challenges can hurt workplace performance through distraction and absenteeism, according to a 2024 report by the TIAA Institute, the research arm of retirement fund manager TIAA. Poor mental health can also impair cognition, making it more difficult to make sound money decisions, the report suggested.

Obsessing over every dollar can also make it feel impossible to part with money for causes that would otherwise align with your personal values, such as charitable giving or support for loved ones, Hargrove says. "And then our money actually comes between us and who we want to be," she adds.

Financial anxiety often results in repetitive behaviors, such as obsessively checking your bank account or stock portfolio, notes Hargrove. For those not in immediate financial peril, Hargrove suggests reminding yourself "enough is enough" and working to replace those behaviors with actions that foster gratitude for the cherished aspects of your life that are not related to your finances.

If you constantly check banking apps on your phone, you might change your wallpaper to a photo of your family or friend group to remind you of the safety of your family and social networks, for instance. It's a simple trick, but it can soon become part of a pattern of "focusing on thoughts of gratitude" that research has shown can lower levels of the stress hormone cortisol in your body, Hargrove says.

And if you can feel like you're using your money to make a positive difference, whether for yourself or others — as Cuban suggested in his post that he does — you probably have a healthier mindset around money than a lot of other Americans, notes Hargrove.

"Living with gratitude and feeling like you make a difference, either for yourself or for other people, [there's] not much better than that in life, right?" Hargrove says.

#2901. NFL odds, betting, storylines: 2 underdogs to back in Week 9

If the NFL betting community handed out a first-half MVP award, there is no question who this year's runaway winner would be: big NFL underdogs.

Through eight weeks, seven teams have taken the field as double-digit underdogs. Five of them covered the spread.

So far, we have seen 37 underdogs of six points or more (including those seven double-digit pups). Their ATS record: 24-10-3 ATS.

This includes a 6-2 ATS mark posted by the largest underdog of the week. In fact, the biggest underdog has covered the spread each of the last five weeks and recorded three outright upsets.

All of which brings me to my trio of NFL underdog best bets for Week 9. Spoiler alert: I expect big 'dogs to continue barking.

Malik Willis (No. 2) and Ollie Gordon II could have a surprise in store for the Lions. (Photo by Megan Briggs/Getty Images)

After an uneven 2-2 start to the season — a start that included a frantic overtime home win against the Saints, blowout road losses to the Bills and Panthers, and zero covers — the Lions have turned things around.

The bounce-back began with a 45-21 thumping of the Cardinals in Arizona. Detroit then followed its bye with consecutive divisional home wins over Green Bay and Minnesota. Those wins, however, were hardly convincing. The Lions needed a last-second field goal to upend the Packers 20-17 and a late-game touchdown and defensive stop to hold off Minnesota 19-15.

So seven games into the 2026 season, Detroit has defeated just two opponents by more than four points: Arizona and the New York Jets (who lost 31-24 in Motown in Week 3). The Lions also enter Week 8 having covered only two point spreads.

Now they head to South Beach in what is a classic letdown/sandwich spot.

The letdown part: Detroit just barely beat two NFC North rivals in tense, down-to-the-wire games (both of which were at home). Now with their confidence sky high, the Lions hop on a plane to face one-win Miami.

The sandwich part: Detroit next week kicks off a three-game homestand against New England. So this is the team's only road trip over a six-game stretch.

My point is that this is a tricky situation for the Lions. Which means it's an advantageous one for the Dolphins, who have been much more competitive over the past month.

Since opening with double-digit losses to the Raiders, 49ers and Chiefs by the combined score of 86-36, Miami has upset the Jets 28-25 on the road and held its own against the Vikings (15-10 road loss as a 10-point underdog), Bengals (31-24 home loss as a 7-point underdog) and Patriots (24-19 home loss as a 7-point underdog).

Yep, Miami comes into this contest on a 3-0-1 ATS run. And while the defense has admittedly struggled most of the season, the offense has picked it up recently. Since tallying 13, 13, 10 and 10 points in their first four games, the Dolphins averaged 23.7 points in the last three.

If the offense can exceed that average Sunday by a tiny bit — against a Detroit defense that I'm not convinced is fixed — Miami will get inside this big number.

In Week 1, the Commanders traveled to Philadelphia as 6-point underdogs. They lost 24-22.

Two weeks later, with injured starting quarterback Jayden Daniels on the sidelines, the Commanders hosted defending champion Seattle as 8.5-point underdogs. They won 33-31.

Fast-forward to Week 6, and Washington traveled to San Francisco as a 7.5-point underdog on Monday Night Football. The 49ers won — but only by six points (23-17).

Those were the three biggest point spreads the Commanders have faced as an underdog this season. And they covered them all. Now they're catching a bunch of points against the visiting Rams, who have looked much better the last two weeks, thumping the Raiders and Chargers by the combined score of 59-30.

But let's not forget that this same Rams squad preceded those two wins with an 18-16 loss to Arizona as a 12.5-point home favorite. Before that, L.A. suffered an overtime home loss to Buffalo; needed a furious late fourth-quarter rally to win at Philadelphia; and blew a 16-0 halftime lead in a 30-26 loss at Denver.

Back to Washington: True, it got crushed 37-20 at Dallas as a 4.5-point underdog in Week 2. But it lost Daniels to an elbow injury right before halftime.

The Commanders also fell 30-13 to the Colts in London as 5-point underdogs in Week 4. But seventh-round rookie quarterback Athan Kaliakmanis played the majority of that game after backup Marcus Mariota went down early.

Daniels finally returned the following week, which was the now-infamous 27-27 tie against the Giants (who are 4-2-1). Then came the competitive loss at then-unbeaten San Francisco, followed by an off week, followed by last Sunday's 27-10 rout of the Eagles.

Bottom line: The Commanders have been blasted only twice this season — once with their backup QB playing the second half and once with their third-string rookie QB playing the majority of the game. Assuming Daniels can stay on the field for 60 minutes Sunday, I like Washington's chances to hang with a West Coast team traveling across the country to play in the early window.

By the way, the Rams' 14-point victory at Las Vegas two weeks ago? It's the only time they've won by more than four points in their last eight games outside of Los Angeles.

#2900. Berkshire Hathaway more than doubles buybacks in third quarter as earnings decline

Berkshire Hathaway said Saturday it accelerated its share repurchases during the third quarter, as operating and overall profits took a hit.

The Greg Abel-led conglomerate bought back $9.6 billion worth of Berkshire stock. That's more than double the $4.5 billion repurchased in the second quarter and brings the company's total buybacks for 2026 to about $14.4 billion.

The sharp increase came as Berkshire's operating earnings — those from wholly-owned businesses — fell year over year, as profits from its insurance business pulled back.

Berkshire operating profits totaled $12.705 billion, down 5.8% from $13.485 billion in the year-earlier period. Insurance underwriting tumbled 43% year on year to $1.352 billion, while insurance investment income declined 14% to $2.734 billion. Berkshire's railroad, energy and manufacturing businesses all saw earnings grow year over year.

The company's overall earnings, which include Berkshire's sprawling stock market portfolio, also dropped 28% in the third quarter to $22.112 billion from $30.796 billion a year prior. Investment gains for the company fell nearly 50% to $9.407 billion.

Despite the earnings pullback, Berkshire stock has outperformed recently amid a massive rotation out of technology stocks. Class A shares are up more than 12% over the past month, while the S&P 500 has dropped 5.5% in that time. Year to date, Berkshire has gained 13%, while the broad market index is up just 7.8%.

Berkshire's five largest equity holdings remained the same in the third quarter: Alphabet, American Express, Apple, Bank of America and Coca-Cola.

Berkshire's cash hoard also grew 3% in the third quarter $377.435 billion from $365.514 billion in the second quarter. However, excluding rail and utility cash along with T-bill payables, the conglomerate's cash holdings totaled $352.3 billion, down 1.9% quarter over quarter.

— CNBC's Alex Crippen contributed reporting.

#2899. Raiders Hoping Ashton Jeanty's Demotion Is 'Wake-Up Call'

The Las Vegas Raiders moved to 4-4 with a 38-33 win on the road against the New York Jets on Sunday.

Kirk Cousins tossed three touchdown passes as No. 1 pick Fernando Mendoza still waits his turn to make his first NFL regular season start.

The biggest surprise was that 2025 first-round pick Ashton Jeanty was demoted to a passing-game role.

Jeanty had just 2 carries for 9 yards while 2026 fourth-round pick Mike Washington Jr. had 25 carries for 83 yards. Jeanty had three receptions for 64 yards while Washington had zero targets, and it was quite the surprising demotion for Jeanty, the team's first-round selection a year ago.

On the year, Jeanty has just 337 rushing yards with 1 score and an average of 3.1 yards per carry, so there have been concerns about his play.

ESPN's Dan Graziano revealed that while the team hoped it was the preseason ankle issues, the fact of the matter is that Las Vegas is hoping this serves as a "wake-up call" for Jeanty more than anything.

"Jeanty hasn't played well while Washington has, and the Raiders hope it serves as a wake-up call," Graziano wrote.

The ESPN insider said the team thought it was the ankle injury that limited Jeanty, but the last few weeks have been very disappointing for the former Boise State star running back.

Jeanty's only 100-yard rushing game of the season came in Week 1 as he had 23 carries for 102 yards against the lowly Miami Dolphins defense.

Since then, he has rushed for 50 or more yards twice, and he has yet to rush for more than 30 yards in any of the last four games, causing the Raiders to try something new.

Another thing regarding this move is that it shows that first-year head coach Klint Kubiak has a lot of decision-making power despite being a rookie wearing the main headset.

Kubiak wasn't around last season when Jeanty was a rookie, and even though the RB was a top-10 pick, he made the choice to demote him.

Whether or not this serves as a wake-up call for Jeanty remains to be seen, but Washington has sure made the most of his usage this season.

The Raiders next face the San Francisco 49ers, followed by the Seattle Seahawks and Denver Broncos in three straight games against talented teams with loaded defenses, so it isn't a great time for either Raiders RB to have the chance to prove themselves.
